🎯 What This Document Is — And How To Use It

This is your complete LFCS exam preparation system. It's not just a study guide — it's a training simulator built around a real Django application (anihpj/jobpost) running on your Linux lab. Every section is designed to build muscle memory through progressive difficulty: theory → troubleshooting → hands-on practice.

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Purpose: Build foundational knowledge across all 5 LFCS domains. Every question has a detailed explanation with real-world context from anihpj/jobpost.

Domains covered: Operations Deployment (Q1-Q50), Networking (Q51-Q100), Storage (Q101-Q140), Essential Commands (Q141-Q180), Users & Groups (Q181-Q200).

How to use: Answer 20-30 questions per study session. Read the explanation even for correct answers — they contain deeper insights.

✅ Daily Study Checklist
  • Morning (30 min): Read 5-10 MCQs from Part 1 + review explanations. Focus on one domain per day.
  • Afternoon (45 min): Practice commands from the Live Shell sections on your Linux VM. Type every command — don't copy-paste.
  • Evening (30 min): Review diagrams and quick-reference cards. Redraw key diagrams from memory (systemd, LVM, networking, etc.).
  • Weekend (2-3 hours): Full review of the week's topics. Run through 50 MCQs back-to-back. Review any weak areas.

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Beginner Pitfalls

  • ❌ "Why is my disk full?" — No idea how to use df/du
  • ❌ "The service won't start" — Don't know journalctl or systemctl
  • ❌ "My config change disappeared after reboot" — Didn't know about /etc/fstab or persistent sysctl
  • ❌ "I can't SSH to my server" — Firewall blocking, sshd not running, or wrong key
  • ❌ "The database keeps crashing" — Never checked memory limits, swap, or OOM killer
  • ❌ "I ran rm -rf and now nothing works" — No backup strategy
  • ❌ "SELinux is blocking my app but I don't know why" — audit2allow concept unknown

✅ AFTER — Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Diagnose any system failure in <2 min using systematic troubleshooting
  • ✅ Configure LVM, resize filesystems, manage swap without downtime
  • ✅ Secure servers with iptables/nftables, SELinux, SSH hardening
  • ✅ Deploy and manage containers with Docker/Podman and systemd
  • ✅ Set up Nginx reverse proxies, load balancers, and SSL termination
  • ✅ Monitor performance with top, iostat, sar, vmstat, and troubleshoot bottlenecks
  • ✅ Manage users, groups, ACLs, LDAP, sudo, and resource limits professionally

🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The Exam
  • ✅ DO: Use man <command> to check syntax — the built-in manual is your best friend.
  • ✅ DO: Run systemctl status <service> and journalctl -u <service> as the FIRST troubleshooting step.
  • ✅ DO: Use find / -name <file> and grep -r <pattern> /etc/ to locate configuration files.
  • ✅ DO: Test with curl localhost:port and nc -zv host port to verify services are responding.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 7 minutes on any single task. Flag it, move on, return with fresh eyes.
  • ❌ DON'T: Forget to make config changes persistent — edit the config file AND restart the service.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any task blank. Even partial work (creating the user, writing partial config) may earn partial credit.
  • ❌ DON'T: Run rm -rf or destructive commands without double-checking your current directory (pwd first!).

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

LFCS Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam Duration: 2 hours | Format: Performance-based (hands-on CLI, Ubuntu) | Passing Score: 66% | Cost: $445 (includes one free retake) | Certification Validity: 2 years

5 Official Domains: Operations Deployment (25%), Networking (25%), Storage (20%), Essential Commands (20%), Users & Groups (10%). Operations Deployment + Networking = 50% of your score — focus your study time accordingly.

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first (5 min) → Solve easy questions first → Flag and return to hard ones → Use man pages and --help liberally → Always verify your work with systemctl status, journalctl, and curl. The exam environment provides full access to man pages and documentation.

Your Linux lab (below) mirrors real exam conditions. Use SSH to access your Ubuntu VMs for hands-on practice.

📚 Pre-Learning: Linux FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Mental Model

F.1 What Is Linux & Why Does It Run the World? — The "operating system" concept. From bare metal to the cloud. Why Linux dominates 90% of public cloud and all top 500 supercomputers.

F.2 The Linux Filesystem — Your "Pantry & Filing Cabinet" — FHS (Filesystem Hierarchy Standard). /etc = control panel, /var = data, /home = workspaces, /tmp = scratch. Where anihpj/jobpost lives: /opt/anihpj/, /etc/anihpj/, /var/log/anihpj/.

F.3 The Shell — Your "Command Center" — bash, PATH, stdin/stdout/stderr, pipes, redirection, man pages. Essential navigation: ls, cd, pwd, mkdir, find, grep.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application Stack — Django (web+API), PostgreSQL (database), Redis (cache), Nginx (reverse proxy). How they run as systemd services on Linux.

F.5 Processes & the Kernel — The "Factory Floor" — PID, PPID, signals, /proc filesystem, strace, systemd as PID 1. How anihpj runs: gunicorn workers, PostgreSQL backends, Nginx workers.

If you're new to Linux: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand the MCQs, troubleshooting issues, and lab scenarios. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example on Linux.

🖥️ Category 1: Operations Deployment (25%)Q1-Q50

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Understanding Linux System Operations

This category is about keeping the system running and recoverable. Think of it like being a building maintenance engineer — you configure the foundation (kernel parameters), manage the workers (processes & services), schedule tasks (cron/systemd timers), install tools (packages), recover from failures (GRUB/fsck), run virtual machines (libvirt), manage containers (Docker/Podman), and enforce security (SELinux). These skills keep ANY production web stack — regardless of language — running reliably on Linux.

Q1-Q50 50 Questions 🟢 Beginner → 🟣 Advanced

1.1 Kernel Parameters — sysctlQ1-Q6

OpsDeploy Q1 of 200
You change a kernel parameter with sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1. After rebooting the server, the setting reverts to 0. Why?
A) Kernel parameters can only be set at compile time and cannot be changed at runtime
B) sysctl -w only changes the runtime value — it does NOT persist across reboots. You must add the setting to /etc/sysctl.conf or /etc/sysctl.d/
C) The kernel automatically resets all parameters to defaults on every reboot for security reasons
D) /proc/sys/ values are read-only and cannot be modified at all
✅ Correct Answer: B

sysctl -w and echo 1 > /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward only modify the runtime kernel parameter in memory. When the system reboots, the kernel reloads all parameters from persistent configuration files — /etc/sysctl.conf and /etc/sysctl.d/*.conf. If your parameter isn't in those files, it's lost.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kernel Parameter Persistence — Runtime vs Boot-Time
  RUNTIME (volatile — lost on reboot)            PERSISTENT (survives reboot)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_fwd=1    │           │ /etc/sysctl.conf             │
  │ echo 1 > /proc/sys/net/ipv4/.. │           │ net.ipv4.ip_forward=1        │
  │                                 │           │                              │
  │ ✅ Immediate effect             │           │ /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj.conf │
  │ ❌ Gone after reboot            │           │ vm.swappiness=10             │
  └─────────────────────────────────┘           │ net.core.somaxconn=1024      │
                                                 │                              │
  TO PERSIST:                                    │ ✅ Applied at boot            │
  echo "net.ipv4.ip_forward=1" >>               │ ✅ sysctl --system reloads    │
    /etc/sysctl.d/99-network.conf                └──────────────────────────────┘
  sysctl --system   ← apply all config files now
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 0
$ sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1

$ sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1 ← Works NOW
$ cat /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward
1 ← Same value, /proc/sys/ is the kernel's view
$ sudo reboot
# ... system reboots ...

$ sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 0 ← GONE after reboot!
$ grep ip_forward /etc/sysctl.conf /etc/sysctl.d/*.conf
# No output — never persisted!
✅ THE FIX — Make It Survive Reboot:

$ echo "net.ipv4.ip_forward=1" | sudo tee /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj.conf
net.ipv4.ip_forward=1

$ sudo sysctl --system
* Applying /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj.conf ...
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1
* Applying /etc/sysctl.conf ...

$ sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1 ✅ Now persists across reboots!

This is critical for any production server: container engines (Docker/Podman) require net.ipv4.ip_forward=1 for container networking. Databases like PostgreSQL or MySQL benefit from vm.swappiness=10 to avoid swapping database pages — swapping kills database performance. Reverse proxies (Nginx/HAProxy) need net.core.somaxconn=1024 for high-traffic connection queues. Always persist kernel parameters or your services will break after the next scheduled reboot — regardless of what language your app is written in.

OpsDeploy Q2 of 200
What is the relationship between /proc/sys/ and the sysctl command?
A) They are completely independent — /proc/sys/ is for reading, sysctl is for writing
B) /proc/sys/ is the legacy interface; sysctl is the modern replacement and they don't interact
C) They are two views of the SAME kernel parameters — /proc/sys/ is the virtual filesystem interface, sysctl is the command-line tool that reads/writes the same values
D) /proc/sys/ is read-only at all times; only sysctl can modify kernel behavior
✅ Correct Answer: C

/proc/sys/ is a virtual filesystem (procfs) that exposes kernel parameters as files. sysctl is the command-line tool that reads and writes these same parameters — they are two interfaces to the identical kernel data. When you run sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward, it's reading /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward. When you echo 1 > /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward, it's the same as sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Two Interfaces, One Kernel — /proc/sys/ vs sysctl
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        USER SPACE                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │   sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward        echo 1 > /proc/sys/net/...  │
  │   (command-line tool)               (filesystem write)           │
  │         │                                    │                   │
  │         └──────────────┬─────────────────────┘                   │
  │                        │                                         │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                        ▼              KERNEL SPACE               │
  │               ┌────────────────┐                                 │
  │               │  /proc/sys/    │  ← Virtual filesystem (procfs)  │
  │               │  (same data)   │     Not real files on disk!     │
  │               └────────┬───────┘                                 │
  │                        │                                         │
  │                        ▼                                         │
  │               ┌────────────────┐                                 │
  │               │  KERNEL PARAMS │  ← The actual kernel variables  │
  │               │  (in memory)   │     Modified in real-time       │
  │               └────────────────┘                                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEY INSIGHT: /proc/sys/ files are 0 bytes on disk (they don't exist!)
  They are generated ON-THE-FLY by the kernel when you read them.
  ls -l /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward → -rw-r--r-- 1 root root 0
  The "0" means no actual file — the kernel creates the content when accessed.
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 0

$ cat /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward
0 ← SAME value!
$ ls -la /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward
-rw-r--r-- 1 root root 0 Jan 1 00:00 /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward
# Size = 0 bytes — virtual file, generated on-the-fly by the kernel!
$ sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1

$ cat /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward
1 ← Both changed — same kernel variable!
$ echo 0 | sudo tee /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward
0

$ sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 0 ← sysctl sees the change too!

For any production server, both interfaces work — sysctl is cleaner for scripts, while /proc/sys/ is useful when you need to check values from within a container or minimal environment where sysctl might not be available.

OpsDeploy Q3 of 200
Where should you add a custom kernel parameter to ensure it survives reboots AND does not get overwritten by future package updates?
A) /etc/sysctl.conf — the main configuration file, edited directly
B) /etc/sysctl.d/99-custom.conf — a dedicated drop-in file that is loaded after all other configs and won't be touched by package updates
C) /proc/sys/ — writing directly to the kernel interface ensures persistence
D) ~/.bashrc — adding sysctl -w commands to run at every login
✅ Correct Answer: B

Option A (/etc/sysctl.conf) works but may be overwritten by package updates. Option B (/etc/sysctl.d/99-custom.conf) is the best practice — drop-in files in /etc/sysctl.d/ are processed in alphabetical order, and a high-numbered file like 99-custom.conf loads LAST, overriding any earlier settings. Package managers never touch files they didn't create.

📝 Detailed Explanation
sysctl Configuration Loading Order — Who Wins?
  ON BOOT (or sysctl --system):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ① /etc/sysctl.d/*.conf     (loaded FIRST, alphabetical order)    │
  │    ├── 10-network.conf      net.ipv4.ip_forward=0                │
  │    ├── 50-security.conf     kernel.randomize_va_space=2          │
  │    └── 99-custom.conf       net.ipv4.ip_forward=1  ← OVERRIDES! │
  │                                                                  │
  │ ② /etc/sysctl.conf          (loaded LAST — legacy location)      │
  │    (This file still works but /etc/sysctl.d/ is preferred)       │
  │                                                                  │
  │ ⚠️  Files in /usr/lib/sysctl.d/ are VENDOR defaults —            │
  │    do NOT edit them. Override in /etc/sysctl.d/ instead.         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ echo "net.ipv4.ip_forward=1" | sudo tee /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj.conf
net.ipv4.ip_forward=1

$ cat /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj.conf
net.ipv4.ip_forward=1
$ sudo sysctl --system
* Applying /etc/sysctl.d/10-network.conf ...
* Applying /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj.conf ...
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1
* Applying /etc/sysctl.conf ...
$ sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1

$ sudo reboot
# ... after reboot ...

$ sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1 ✅ Survived reboot!
$ ls /etc/sysctl.d/
10-network.conf 50-security.conf 99-anihpj.conf README.sysctl
# 99-anihpj.conf loads LAST — overrides any earlier settings

For production deployments, always use /etc/sysctl.d/ drop-in files. This keeps your custom settings isolated, easy to backup (tar czf sysctl-backup.tar.gz /etc/sysctl.d/), and safe from package upgrades that might overwrite /etc/sysctl.conf. Use high numbers (80-99) to ensure your settings take precedence.

OpsDeploy Q4 of 200
Which combination of kernel parameters is appropriate for a production server running PostgreSQL and Nginx?
A) vm.swappiness=60 and net.core.somaxconn=128 — the Linux defaults work fine for everything
B) vm.swappiness=0 and net.ipv4.ip_forward=0 — disable swap entirely and block all forwarding
C) net.ipv4.tcp_tw_recycle=1 and vm.overcommit_memory=2 — aggressive TCP recycling with strict memory accounting
D) vm.swappiness=10 (reduce swap tendency for database) and net.core.somaxconn=1024 (increase connection backlog for web server)
✅ Correct Answer: D

vm.swappiness=10 tells the kernel to avoid swapping unless absolutely necessary — critical for PostgreSQL because database pages in swap destroy query performance. net.core.somaxconn=1024 increases the maximum number of pending connections, essential for Nginx under load. The defaults (swappiness=60, somaxconn=128) are tuned for desktop use, not production servers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Key Production Kernel Parameters — What They Do
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PARAMETER                  DEFAULT    PRODUCTION   WHY?            │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  vm.swappiness              60         10           Less swapping   │
  │    → 0-100. Lower = avoid swap. PostgreSQL + swap = SLOW queries   │
  │                                                                     │
  │  net.core.somaxconn          128        1024         More backlog   │
  │    → Max pending connections. Nginx needs this for traffic spikes  │
  │                                                                     │
  │  net.ipv4.ip_forward         0          1            Container NAT  │
  │    → Required by Docker/Podman for container networking            │
  │                                                                     │
  │  vm.overcommit_memory        0          1            Redis safety   │
  │    → 0=heuristic, 1=always allow, 2=strict. Redis needs 1 for BGSAVE│
  │                                                                     │
  │  fs.file-max                 9223372    2097152      More file handles│
  │    → System-wide max open files. High-traffic servers need this    │
  │                                                                     │
  │  net.ipv4.tcp_fastopen       1          3            Faster HTTP    │
  │    → 0=off, 1=client, 2=server, 3=both. Reduces TLS handshake RTT │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ sysctl vm.swappiness
vm.swappiness = 60 ← Default (desktop tuning)

$ cat /proc/sys/vm/swappiness
60
$ cat /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj-prod.conf
# PostgreSQL — avoid swapping database pages
vm.swappiness = 10
# Nginx — handle traffic spikes
net.core.somaxconn = 1024
# Docker — enable container networking
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1
# General — increase file handle limit
fs.file-max = 2097152
$ sudo sysctl --system
* Applying /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj-prod.conf ...
vm.swappiness = 10
net.core.somaxconn = 1024
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1
fs.file-max = 2097152

$ sysctl vm.swappiness net.core.somaxconn
vm.swappiness = 10
net.core.somaxconn = 1024 ✅ Production-ready!

For a production web stack: vm.swappiness=10 keeps database pages in RAM where they belong (critical for PostgreSQL or MySQL performance), net.core.somaxconn=1024 ensures your reverse proxy doesn't drop connections under traffic spikes, and net.ipv4.ip_forward=1 enables Docker/Podman networking for containerized deployments.

OpsDeploy Q5 of 200
You need to check which kernel modules are currently loaded and then load the nfs module. Which commands do you use?
A) modlist to list modules, then insmod nfs to load it
B) lsmod to list loaded modules, then modprobe nfs to load the module (handling dependencies automatically)
C) cat /proc/modules to list, then systemctl start nfs to load
D) dkms status to list modules, then apt install nfs-kernel-module to load it
✅ Correct Answer: B

lsmod lists all currently loaded kernel modules (it reads /proc/modules in a formatted way). modprobe is the intelligent module loader — it automatically loads any dependencies first, unlike insmod which requires you to manually load every dependency in the correct order. Always use modprobe over insmod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kernel Module Management — The Tool Hierarchy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  lsmod        List all loaded modules (formatted /proc/modules) │
  │  modprobe     Smart loader — handles DEPENDENCIES automatically │
  │  modinfo      Show module details (path, license, dependencies) │
  │  rmmod        Remove a module (only if unused)                  │
  │  modprobe -r  Remove module AND its unused dependencies         │
  │  insmod       Low-level loader — NO dependency resolution!      │
  │               (Avoid — use modprobe instead)                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  MODULE DEPENDENCY EXAMPLE:                                     │
  │  nfs → nfsv4 → sunrpc → lockd → nfs_acl                        │
  │  modprobe nfs loads ALL of these automatically.                 │
  │  insmod nfs.ko would FAIL unless you loaded all 4 deps first!   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ lsmod | head -10
Module Size Used by
nfs 311296 0
nfsv4 479232 1 nfs
dns_resolver 16384 1 nfsv4
ext4 950272 2
mbcache 16384 1 ext4
dm_crypt 53248 0
... # "Used by" column shows dependencies
$ modinfo nfs
filename: /lib/modules/6.8.0-31-generic/kernel/fs/nfs/nfs.ko
license: GPL
depends: nfsv4,dns_resolver
description: NFS client
$ sudo modprobe nfs
# No output = success! Module and all dependencies loaded.

$ lsmod | grep nfs
nfs 311296 0
nfsv4 479232 1 nfs
dns_resolver 16384 1 nfsv4 ✅ All dependencies loaded automatically!
$ sudo modprobe -r nfs
# Removes nfs + any unused dependencies

$ cat /etc/modprobe.d/blacklist.conf
# Security: disable rarely-used network protocols
blacklist dccp
blacklist sctp
blacklist rds
blacklist tipc

For production servers: NFS modules are needed if you mount shared storage. Security best practice is to blacklist unused protocols like DCCP and SCTP in /etc/modprobe.d/ — they're rarely needed and expand the attack surface. Always use modprobe over insmod to avoid dependency ordering nightmares.

OpsDeploy Q6 of 200
You are deploying a production web stack (app server + database + cache + reverse proxy) on an Ubuntu server. Which /etc/sysctl.d/ configuration is the most complete and appropriate set of kernel tunings?
A) Only net.ipv4.ip_forward=1 — that's all Docker needs; the defaults handle everything else fine
B) vm.swappiness=0 and vm.overcommit_memory=2 — aggressive memory management prevents all OOM issues
C) vm.swappiness=10 (PostgreSQL), net.core.somaxconn=1024 (Nginx), net.ipv4.ip_forward=1 (Docker), vm.overcommit_memory=1 (Redis), fs.file-max=2097152 (general)
D) kernel.hostname=anihpj-prod and net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control=bbr — hostname and TCP algorithm are the only tunables that matter
✅ Correct Answer: C

Each parameter targets a specific component of a production web stack. vm.swappiness=10 keeps database pages in RAM, net.core.somaxconn=1024 prevents the reverse proxy from dropping connections under load, net.ipv4.ip_forward=1 enables container networking, vm.overcommit_memory=1 allows Redis (or any in-memory cache) to fork for background saves, and fs.file-max=2097152 ensures the system has enough file handles for all services combined.

  │       │                                   │                    │      │
  │       ▼                                   ▼                    ▼      │
  │  somaxconn=1024                    swappiness=10       overcommit=1   │
  │  "Don't drop conns"              "Keep DB in RAM"    "Allow BGSAVE"   │
  │                                                                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Docker/Podman Containers                                       │  │
  │  │  net.ipv4.ip_forward=1  ←  "Enable container networking"       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │

  │  │  System-Wide                                                    │  │
  │  │  fs.file-max=2097152  ←  "Enough handles for all 4 services"   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This is the complete kernel tuning baseline for any production web server. Always test in staging first — while these values are safe defaults, your workload may need adjustments. 1.2 Processes & ServicesQ7-Q14

OpsDeploy Q7 of 200
You need to find the Process ID (PID) of the app server process running the anihpj-api web application. Which command gives you the most complete snapshot of all processes including PID, CPU, memory, and the full command line?
A) ps aux | grep gunicorn — BSD-style listing showing every process with USER, PID, %CPU, %MEM, and full command
B) ls /proc/ | grep gunicorn — the /proc directory lists all running process IDs directly
C) pidof gunicorn — returns just the PID, which is all you ever need
D) systemctl show anihpj-api | grep PID — systemd maintains the authoritative process registry
✅ Correct Answer: A

ps aux is the universal process listing command on Linux. a = show processes from all users (not just yours), u = user-oriented format (USER, %CPU, %MEM, VSZ, RSS), x = include processes without a controlling terminal (daemons like app server workers, database backends, cache processes). Piping through grep filters to only the processes you care about.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ps aux — Decoding Every Column
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  USER    PID   %CPU %MEM    VSZ    RSS TTY  STAT START   TIME COMMAND    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  anihpj  1234   0.3  2.1  285000 52000 ?    Ssl  08:30   0:12 gunicorn  │
  │  anihpj  1235   0.1  1.8  282000 45000 ?    Sl   08:30   0:05 gunicorn  │
  │  anihpj  1236   0.2  1.9  283000 47000 ?    Sl   08:30   0:08 gunicorn  │
  │                                                                          │
  │  KEY COLUMNS:                                                            │
  │  USER    = Who owns this process (anihpj app user)                       │
  │  PID     = Process ID (unique number)                                    │
  │  %CPU    = CPU usage percentage                                          │
  │  %MEM    = Physical memory usage percentage                              │
  │  VSZ/RSS = Virtual/Resident memory in KB                                 │
  │  STAT    = Process state: S=sleeping, R=running, s=session leader,       │
  │            l=multi-threaded, +=foreground process group                   │
  │  START   = When the process started                                      │
  │  TIME    = Total CPU time used                                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ ps aux | grep gunicorn | grep -v grep
anihpj 1234 0.3 2.1 285000 52000 ? Ssl 08:30 0:12 /opt/anihpj/venv/bin/gunicorn anihpj.wsgi:application --bind 0.0.0.0:8000 --workers 3
anihpj 1235 0.1 1.8 282000 45000 ? Sl 08:30 0:05 /opt/anihpj/venv/bin/gunicorn anihpj.wsgi:application --bind 0.0.0.0:8000 --workers 3
anihpj 1236 0.2 1.9 283000 47000 ? Sl 08:30 0:08 /opt/anihpj/venv/bin/gunicorn anihpj.wsgi:application --bind 0.0.0.0:8000 --workers 3
$ ps aux --sort=-%mem | head -5
USER PID %CPU %MEM VSZ RSS TTY STAT START TIME COMMAND
postgres 890 0.5 12.3 450000 310000 ? Ss 08:00 0:45 /usr/lib/postgresql/15/bin/postgres
anihpj 1234 0.3 2.1 285000 52000 ? Ssl 08:30 0:12 gunicorn: master
# --sort=-%mem = top memory consumers first (anihpj DB = heaviest!)
$ ps -ef | grep postgres | head -3
postgres 890 1 0 08:00 ? 00:00:45 /usr/lib/postgresql/15/bin/postgres -D /var/lib/postgresql/15/main
# -ef = Unix-style: UID PID PPID (parent PID) C STIME TTY TIME CMD

For production troubleshooting: ps aux | grep app-server shows if your application is running, how many workers are active, and their memory usage. The master process (PID 1234 in the example) is the one to send signals to for graceful restart: kill -HUP 1234. This pattern works identically whether your app uses gunicorn (Python), puma (Ruby), npm start (Node.js), or a Go binary.

OpsDeploy Q8 of 200
You suspect your web application is consuming excessive CPU. Which tool provides a real-time, interactive view of processes sorted by CPU usage, and can also display a process tree showing parent-child relationships?
A) ps aux --sort=-%cpu — a one-time snapshot sorted by CPU is sufficient for all monitoring
B) vmstat 1 — system-wide CPU statistics updated every second
C) htop — an interactive process viewer with color-coded CPU/memory bars, process tree view (F5), and mouse support for sorting and killing
D) /proc/loadavg — the kernel's load average is the only reliable CPU metric
✅ Correct Answer: C

htop is the interactive, human-friendly version of top. It shows color-coded bars for CPU and memory, supports mouse clicks, and pressing F5 switches to tree view — showing exactly which worker was spawned by which master process. ps aux is a static snapshot, vmstat shows system-wide stats (not per-process), and /proc/loadavg is just a single number.

📝 Detailed Explanation
htop Interface — What You See
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1  [|||||||||||||||||||  25.3%]   Tasks: 45, 12 thr; 1 running│
  │  2  [||||||||||||         15.1%]   Load average: 0.32 0.28 0.25 │
  │  Mem [||||||||||||||||||||||||||||||||||||||||||||||| 2.1G/3.8G]│
  │  Swp [                                                  0K/2.0G]│
  │                                                                 │
  │  PID   USER   PRI  NI  VIRT   RES   SHR  CPU% MEM%  TIME+  Cmd │
  │  1234  anihpj  20   0  285M  52.0M  12M   0.3  2.1  0:12  gunic│
  │  1235  anihpj  20   0  282M  45.0M  12M   0.1  1.8  0:05  gunic│
  │   890  postgr  20   0  450M  310M   25M   0.5 12.3  0:45  postgr│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEY BINDINGS:
  F5 = Tree view     F6 = Sort by column     F9 = Kill process
  F2 = Setup         F3 = Search             F10 = Quit
  P  = Sort by CPU   M = Sort by memory      T = Sort by time
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action

# Opens interactive htop (press F5 for tree view, F10 to quit)

# Monitor only those 3 PIDs (gunicorn master + 2 workers)
$ top -p $(pgrep -d',' gunicorn)
# Classic top, monitoring only gunicorn processes

$ top -b -n 1 | head -5
