Tasks: 156 total, 1 running, 155 sleeping, 0 stopped, 0 zombie
%Cpu(s): 2.3 us, 1.1 sy, 0.0 ni, 96.1 id, 0.3 wa, 0.0 hi, 0.2 si
# -b = batch mode (non-interactive), -n 1 = one iteration — great for scripts!

Use htop interactively to spot which app worker is stuck at 100% CPU. Use top -b -n 1 in cron scripts for automated CPU monitoring. The tree view (F5 in htop) is invaluable for understanding the master → worker parent-child hierarchy — whether you're running gunicorn (Python), puma (Ruby), PM2 (Node.js), or any other process manager.

OpsDeploy Q9 of 200
You need to gracefully reload Nginx after updating its configuration. Instead of finding the PID manually with ps, which single command finds and signals all Nginx processes?
A) kill -9 nginx — kill accepts process names directly
B) pkill -HUP nginx — finds all processes named "nginx" and sends the SIGHUP (reload configuration) signal
C) systemctl signal nginx HUP — systemd is the only safe way to signal services
D) killall -9 nginx — killall is the recommended tool for production reloads
✅ Correct Answer: B

pkill (process kill) finds processes by name pattern and sends them a signal — no need to look up PIDs manually. pkill -HUP nginx sends SIGHUP (hangup), which tells Nginx to reload its configuration gracefully without dropping connections. pgrep nginx would show you which PIDs matched, and pidof nginx returns just the PIDs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Process Lookup Tools — When to Use Each
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TOOL      │  WHAT IT DOES              │  EXAMPLE              │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │  pgrep     │  Find PIDs by name/pattern │  pgrep -u anihpj      │
  │            │  Returns: list of PIDs     │  → 1234 1235 1236     │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │  pkill     │  Signal processes by name  │  pkill -HUP nginx     │
  │            │  (pgrep + kill combined)   │  → reloads nginx      │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │  pidof     │  Find PID of a binary      │  pidof gunicorn       │
  │            │  (exact name match only)   │  → 1234 1235 1236     │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │  killall   │  Kill by name (ALL matches)│  killall -HUP nginx   │
  │            │  (similar to pkill, older) │  → reloads all nginx  │
  └────────────┴────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  pgrep FLAGS:  -u USER (by owner)  -f (match full command line)
                -l (show process name)  -d, (comma-separated output)
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ pgrep -u anihpj
1234
1235
1236
# All processes owned by the anihpj user (gunicorn workers)
$ pgrep -f gunicorn
1234
1235
1236
# -f matches against the FULL command line, not just process name
$ pgrep -l -u postgres
890 postgres
891 postgres
892 postgres
# -l shows process name next to PID
$ pkill -HUP nginx
# No output = success! Nginx reloads its config gracefully.

$ systemctl status nginx | head -3
● nginx.service - A high performance web server
Loaded: loaded
Active: active (running)
# Nginx reloaded, no connections dropped! ✅

Use pkill -HUP nginx after updating reverse proxy configs, and pkill -HUP app-server (sends SIGHUP to the master process, which gracefully restarts workers). Never use pkill -9 (SIGKILL) for reloads — it force-kills without cleanup and will drop active connections. This applies regardless of your app's language or framework.

OpsDeploy Q10 of 200
The anihpj-api web application process (PID 1234) is stuck and needs to be terminated. You first try kill 1234 but it doesn't respond. What is the correct escalation sequence for terminating a stubborn process?
A) kill 1234 (SIGTERM — polite, allows cleanup) → wait → kill -9 1234 (SIGKILL — force, no cleanup) as a LAST resort
B) kill -9 1234 immediately — SIGKILL is always the fastest and safest option
C) kill -STOP 1234 then kill -CONT 1234 — toggling the process state forces it to exit
D) reboot — if kill doesn't work, only a full system restart can clear the process
✅ Correct Answer: A

The golden rule of process termination: SIGTERM (15) first, SIGKILL (9) only as a last resort. SIGTERM politely asks the process to shut down — it can clean up (close database connections, flush logs, remove lock files). SIGKILL is the kernel forcibly destroying the process — no cleanup, no warning, no chance to save state. The escalation is: kill PID (SIGTERM) → wait → kill -2 PID (SIGINT, like Ctrl+C) → kill -9 PID (SIGKILL, absolute last resort).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Linux Signals — The Process Communication System
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SIGNAL  │  #  │  ACTION      │  CAN BE CAUGHT? │  USE CASE     │
  ├──────────┼─────┼──────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │  SIGHUP  │  1  │  Terminate   │  YES            │  Reload config│
  │  SIGINT  │  2  │  Terminate   │  YES            │  Ctrl+C       │
  │  SIGQUIT │  3  │  Core dump   │  YES            │  Ctrl+\       │
  │  SIGKILL │  9  │  Terminate   │  NO (kernel)    │  LAST RESORT  │
  │  SIGTERM │ 15  │  Terminate   │  YES            │  Default kill │
  │  SIGSTOP │ 19  │  Stop        │  NO (kernel)    │  Pause proc   │
  │  SIGCONT │ 18  │  Continue    │  YES            │  Resume proc  │
  └──────────┴─────┴──────────────┴─────────────────┴───────────────┘
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ kill 1234
# Sends SIGTERM (15) — "Please stop and clean up"

$ ps -p 1234
PID TTY STAT TIME COMMAND
1234 ? S 0:12 gunicorn: master
# Still running — process is ignoring or blocking SIGTERM
$ kill -2 1234
# Sends SIGINT (2) — same as Ctrl+C in terminal

$ ps -p 1234
1234 ? S 0:12 gunicorn: master
# Still stuck — time for the nuclear option
$ kill -9 1234
# SIGKILL (9) — kernel forcefully destroys the process. No cleanup.

$ ps -p 1234
PID TTY STAT TIME COMMAND
# (no output) — process is gone
$ kill -l
1) SIGHUP 2) SIGINT 3) SIGQUIT 4) SIGILL 5) SIGTRAP
6) SIGABRT 7) SIGBUS 8) SIGFPE 9) SIGKILL 10) SIGUSR1
11) SIGSEGV 12) SIGUSR2 13) SIGPIPE 14) SIGALRM 15) SIGTERM
...
# kill -l lists ALL 64 signals with their numbers

When restarting an app server, always use kill -HUP $(pgrep -f app-server) (SIGHUP = graceful worker restart). During deployments, systemd handles the signal escalation automatically via systemctl restart anihpj-api. Never SIGKILL a database process (PostgreSQL, MySQL, etc.) — you risk data corruption. Always SIGTERM first and give it time to flush and checkpoint.

OpsDeploy Q11 of 200
The anihpj-api gunicorn process is hanging and you need to see exactly which system call it's blocked on. Which tool provides a real-time trace of every syscall a process makes?
A) ltrace gunicorn — traces library calls which are more useful than syscalls
B) journalctl -u anihpj-api --follow — systemd journal already logs all syscalls
C) strace -p $(pgrep gunicorn) — attaches to the running process and prints every kernel syscall with arguments and return values in real time
D) /proc/PID/syscall — the kernel exposes the current syscall of any process in this virtual file
✅ Correct Answer: C

strace is the syscall tracer — it intercepts every system call a process makes (open, read, write, connect, etc.) and prints the call name, arguments, and return value. This is invaluable for debugging "stuck" processes — you can see exactly which syscall they're blocked on. strace -p PID attaches to an already-running process.

📝 Detailed Explanation
strace — What It Captures (Example: App Server Opening a File)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ strace -e open,read,write gunicorn                          │
  │                                                                 │
  │  open("/opt/anihpj/media/resume.pdf", O_RDONLY) = 3             │
  │  │      │                              │         └─ fd returned │
  │  │      │                              └─ flags                 │
  │  │      └─ argument (the file path)                             │
  │  └─ syscall name                                                │
  │                                                                 │
  │  read(3, "...file contents...", 4096) = 512                     │
  │       │                        │        └─ bytes actually read  │
  │       │                        └─ buffer size requested         │
  │       └─ file descriptor                                        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  COMMON FLAGS:  -p PID (attach)  -e trace=open,read (filter)   │
  │                 -c (summary count)  -o file (output to file)    │
  │                 -f (follow child processes)  -t (timestamps)    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ sudo strace -p $(pgrep -f "gunicorn.*master") 2>&1 | head -5
strace: Process 1234 attached
select(12, [5 8 10], NULL, NULL, {tv_sec=14, tv_usec=342000}) = 0 (Timeout)
wait4(-1, 0x7ffc1234, WNOHANG, NULL) = 0
select(12, [5 8 10], NULL, NULL, {tv_sec=14, tv_usec=342000}) = ? ERESTARTNOHAND
# The process is blocked on select() — waiting for I/O
$ sudo strace -e open,read -p $(pgrep -f "gunicorn.*master")
open("/opt/anihpj/settings.py", O_RDONLY) = 4
read(4, "DEBUG = False\nDATABASES = {\n...", 4096) = 1024
open("/etc/anihpj/env", O_RDONLY) = 5
# Filters to only show open() and read() syscalls
$ sudo strace -c -p $(pgrep -f "gunicorn.*master")
# ... let it run for 10 seconds, then Ctrl+C ...
% time seconds usecs/call calls errors syscall
85.23 0.823400 8234 100 select
10.12 0.097800 23 4200 read
4.65 0.044900 89 500 write
# -c = summary mode — 85% of time is spent in select() (I/O wait)

For production troubleshooting: strace -p $(pgrep app-server) is your first diagnostic tool when your application appears hung. If you see it blocked on connect() to port 5432 — the database is unreachable. If blocked on select() — waiting for client requests (normal idle). Use strace -c to identify which syscall dominates (performance profiling). This works identically whether your app uses gunicorn (Python), puma (Ruby), npm start (Node.js), or a Go binary.

OpsDeploy Q12 of 200
You need to find out which process is using port 5432 (PostgreSQL) on the anihpj server. Which command shows this directly?
A) netstat -tlnp | grep 5432 — netstat is the traditional tool for port-to-process mapping
B) lsof -i :5432 — lists open files (including network sockets) and shows exactly which process and PID is using port 5432
C) ss -tlnp | grep 5432 — socket statistics show listening ports but not process details
D) cat /proc/net/tcp | grep 153E — the kernel's TCP table has port mappings in hex
✅ Correct Answer: B

lsof (LiSt Open Files) shows every file, socket, pipe, and device a process has open. The -i :5432 flag filters to network connections on port 5432. It shows the COMMAND, PID, USER, and whether it's listening (LISTEN) or established. ss also works and is faster, but lsof gives more context about the process.

📝 Detailed Explanation
lsof — The Universal "What's Open?" Tool
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  lsof COMMANDS:                                                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  lsof -i :5432        → Who's on PostgreSQL port?               │
  │  lsof -i TCP:8000     → Who's on app server port?               │
  │  lsof -p 1234          → Everything process 1234 has open       │
  │  lsof -u anihpj        → All files opened by user anihpj        │
  │  lsof /var/log/anihpj/app.log  → Which process writes here?   │
  │  lsof +D /opt/anihpj/  → All open files under this directory    │
  │  lsof | grep deleted   → Deleted files still held open (leaks!) │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ sudo lsof -i :5432
COMMAND PID USER FD TYPE DEVICE SIZE/OFF NODE NAME
postgres 890 postgres 5u IPv4 23456 0t0 TCP *:5432 (LISTEN)
postgres 891 postgres 7u IPv4 23478 0t0 TCP localhost:5432->localhost:45678 (ESTABLISHED)
# PID 890 is LISTENING, PID 891 has an active connection from app server
$ sudo lsof -i :8000
COMMAND PID USER FD TYPE DEVICE SIZE/OFF NODE NAME
gunicorn 1234 anihpj 5u IPv4 34567 0t0 TCP *:8000 (LISTEN)
# App server is listening on port 8000 ✅
$ sudo lsof -p 1234 | wc -l
67
# App server master process has 67 open files/sockets/pipes

For production troubleshooting: lsof -i :5432 confirms your database is running and LISTENING. lsof -i :8000 confirms your app server is serving. lsof | grep deleted helps find disk space leaks — log files deleted while still held open by a running process. These commands work across ALL languages and frameworks.

OpsDeploy Q13 of 200
After editing the anihpj-api systemd unit file, you run systemctl start anihpj-api but it fails. What is the first command you should run to diagnose WHY it failed?
A) systemctl status anihpj-api and journalctl -u anihpj-api — shows the current state, exit code, and the last 10 log lines directly in the status output
B) systemctl show anihpj-api — dumps all 200+ properties of the unit in key=value format
C) /opt/anihpj/venv/bin/gunicorn --check-config — the application itself is the only source of truth
D) reboot — a clean boot state ensures all services start correctly
✅ Correct Answer: A

systemctl status anihpj-api is the universal first diagnostic step for any failed systemd service. It shows: Active state (active/inactive/failed), exit code, process PID, and automatically includes the last 10 lines from journalctl — giving you the error message immediately. Then journalctl -u anihpj-api gives the full log history.

💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ sudo systemctl start anihpj-api
Job for anihpj-api.service failed because the control process exited with error code.

$ systemctl status anihpj-api
● anihpj-api.service - anihpj API Server
Loaded: loaded (/etc/systemd/system/anihpj-api.service; enabled)
Active: failed (Result: exit-code)
Process: 5678 ExecStart=/opt/anihpj/venv/bin/gunicorn (code=exited, status=1/FAILURE)
Main PID: 5678 (code=exited, status=1/FAILURE)
$ journalctl -u anihpj-api --since "2 min ago"
gunicorn: error: unrecognized arguments: --workres 3
# TYPO! Should be --workers 3, not --workres 3
$ sudo systemctl edit anihpj-api
# Opens editor for a drop-in override file
# Fix: --workers 3

$ sudo systemctl daemon-reload && sudo systemctl start anihpj-api

$ systemctl status anihpj-api
● anihpj-api.service - anihpj API Server
Active: active (running) ✅

For production troubleshooting: Always check systemctl status first — it shows the exit code and error inline. Then journalctl -u anihpj-api for the full story. Common failures: typos in ExecStart, missing WorkingDirectory, wrong User/Group, or the application binary path is incorrect. These patterns are identical whether your ExecStart runs gunicorn, npm, java -jar, or a Go binary.

📝 systemctl Quick Reference
systemctl — Essential Service Management
  COMMAND                          EFFECT
  ───────────────────────────────  ──────────────────────────────────
  systemctl start/stop/restart X   Start, stop, or restart service
  systemctl enable/disable X       Auto-start at boot / don't
  systemctl enable --now X         Enable AND start in one command
  systemctl status X               Status + last 10 log lines
  systemctl is-active X            Quick check for scripts
  systemctl list-units --failed    Show all failed services
  systemctl daemon-reload          Reload after editing unit files
  systemctl edit X                 Create drop-in override (safe!)
OpsDeploy Q14 of 200
The anihpj-api service has been running for 3 days. You need to see ONLY the logs from the last 30 minutes, with timestamps. Which command does this?
A) tail -f /var/log/anihpj/app.log — traditional log files are more reliable than journald
B) journalctl --unit anihpj-api --tail 100 — shows the last 100 lines regardless of time
C) journalctl -u anihpj-api --since today — shows all logs from midnight
D) journalctl -u anihpj-api --since "30 min ago" --timestamps — filters to the last 30 minutes with human-readable timestamps on every line
✅ Correct Answer: D

journalctl is systemd's unified logging system. -u anihpj-api filters to this specific service. --since "30 min ago" is a powerful relative time filter (also supports "1 hour ago", "yesterday", "2026-06-13"). --timestamps adds human-readable timestamps ([Jun 13 14:30:52]) instead of just the boot-relative time.

💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ journalctl -u anihpj-api --since "30 min ago" --timestamps
Jun 13 14:30:52 anihpj-prod gunicorn[1234]: [INFO] Starting gunicorn 21.2.0
Jun 13 14:30:52 anihpj-prod gunicorn[1234]: [INFO] Listening at: http://0.0.0.0:8000
Jun 13 14:30:52 anihpj-prod gunicorn[1234]: [INFO] Using worker: sync
Jun 13 14:30:53 anihpj-prod gunicorn[1235]: [INFO] Booting worker with pid: 1235
Jun 13 14:50:12 anihpj-prod gunicorn[1234]: [WARNING] Worker timeout (pid: 1235)
$ journalctl -u anihpj-api -u postgresql --since "1 hour ago"
# Show BOTH app server + database logs together — correlate errors!
$ journalctl -u anihpj-api --since "2026-06-13" --until "2026-06-13 15:00"
# Absolute date/time range — precise window for incident investigation
$ journalctl --disk-usage
Archived and active journals take up 512.0M on disk.

For production troubleshooting: journalctl -u anihpj-api -u postgresql --since "1 hour ago" correlates app server and database logs side-by-side — invaluable when debugging "app can't connect to database" errors. Set journalctl --vacuum-size=500M in cron to prevent the journal from filling the disk. This technique works regardless of your app's language.

📝 journalctl Quick Reference
journalctl — systemd's Unified Log Viewer
  COMMAND                                      EFFECT
  ────────────────────────────────────────────  ──────────────────────────
  journalctl -u UNIT                           Logs for one service
  journalctl -u UNIT1 -u UNIT2                  Correlate 2+ services
  journalctl --since "30 min ago"               Relative time filter
  journalctl --since "2026-06-13" --until "..."  Absolute date range
  journalctl -f                                Follow (like tail -f)
  journalctl -p err                            Priority filter (emerg..debug)
  journalctl -o json-pretty                    Machine-readable output
  journalctl --disk-usage                      How much disk space used
  journalctl --vacuum-size=500M                 Limit journal size
  journalctl --vacuum-time=7d                   Keep only 7 days of logs

1.3 Job Scheduling — cron, at & systemd TimersQ15-Q20

OpsDeploy Q15 of 200
You need a script at /opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh to run every day at 2:00 AM. Which crontab entry is correct?
A) 2 0 * * * /opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh — minute 2, hour 0 (2:00 AM)
B) 0 2 * * * /opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh — minute 0, hour 2, every day, every month, every weekday
C) * * 2 0 * /opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh — the day-of-month and hour positions are flexible
D) 0 2 * * 0 /opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh — runs every Sunday at 2AM only
✅ Correct Answer: B

The crontab format is: minute hour day-of-month month day-of-week command. 0 2 * * * means "at minute 0 of hour 2, every day, every month, every weekday" — i.e., daily at 2:00 AM. Each * field acts as a wildcard matching all values.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Crontab Format — Decoding Every Field
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # .---------------- minute (0 - 59)                            │
  │  # │  .------------- hour (0 - 23)                              │
  │  # │  │  .---------- day of month (1 - 31)                      │
  │  # │  │  │  .------- month (1 - 12) OR jan,feb,mar,apr...       │
  │  # │  │  │  │  .---- day of week (0 - 6) (Sunday=0 or 7)        │
  │  # │  │  │  │  │                                                │
  │    0  2  *  *  *    /opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  COMMON PATTERNS:                                               │
  │  */5 * * * *      = Every 5 minutes                             │
  │  0 2 * * *        = Daily at 2:00 AM                            │
  │  0 2 * * 0        = Weekly (Sunday at 2AM)                      │
  │  0 2 1 * *        = Monthly (1st of month at 2AM)               │
  │  0 9-17 * * 1-5   = Every hour 9AM-5PM, Mon-Fri                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️  CRON PITFALLS:                                              │
  │  • cron uses /bin/sh, not bash — use full paths!                │
  │  • PATH in cron is minimal — always use absolute paths          │
  │  • Output is EMAILED to the user unless redirected > /dev/null  │
  │  • % character is special (newline) — escape as \%              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ crontab -l
# Production — Nightly database backup
0 2 * * * /opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh >> /var/log/anihpj/backup.log 2>&1
# Health check every 5 minutes
*/5 * * * * /opt/anihpj/scripts/healthcheck.sh
$ crontab -e
# Opens editor to modify your crontab

$ grep CRON /var/log/syslog | tail -3
Jun 13 02:00:01 anihpj CRON[8901]: (anihpj) CMD (/opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh)
# Confirms the backup ran at 2:00 AM ✅

For production web stacks: Schedule daily database backups at 2AM (pg_dump or mysqldump), health checks every 5 minutes (curl localhost:8000/api/health), and log rotation monthly. Always redirect output to a log file (>> /var/log/) so you can verify execution later.

OpsDeploy Q16 of 200
Instead of editing a user's crontab, you want to drop a script that runs daily without any crontab syntax. Where do you place it?
A) /etc/cron.daily/ — any executable script placed here runs once per day automatically
B) /usr/local/bin/ — scripts in this directory are auto-scheduled by the kernel
C) ~/.local/share/cron/ — freedesktop.org standard for per-user cron directories
D) /etc/crontab — append the script path directly as a new line
✅ Correct Answer: A

/etc/cron.daily/, /etc/cron.hourly/, /etc/cron.weekly/, and /etc/cron.monthly/ are directory-based cron. Just drop an executable script with the right permissions — no crontab syntax needed. These are managed by /etc/crontab via run-parts.

💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ sudo tee /etc/cron.daily/anihpj-cleanup << 'EOF'
> #!/bin/bash
> find /opt/anihpj/media/tmp -type f -mtime +7 -delete
> echo "$(date): Cleaned old temp files" >> /var/log/anihpj/cleanup.log
> EOF

$ sudo chmod +x /etc/cron.daily/anihpj-cleanup

$ run-parts --test /etc/cron.daily/
/etc/cron.daily/anihpj-cleanup
# --test shows which scripts WOULD run (dry-run)

Use /etc/cron.daily/ for cleanup scripts (old uploads, stale sessions, temporary files). The scripts are managed by anacron so they run even if the server was off at the scheduled time — they'll execute shortly after boot. This is a language-agnostic pattern that works for any application.

📝 Cron Directory Quick Reference
/etc/cron.* — Directory-Based Cron Schedule
  DIRECTORY            RUNS EVERY         USE FOR
  ───────────────────  ─────────────────  ────────────────────────────
  /etc/cron.hourly/    Hourly             Health checks, ping monitors
  /etc/cron.daily/     Daily              Cleanups, log summaries
  /etc/cron.weekly/    Weekly             Report generation, audits
  /etc/cron.monthly/   Monthly            Archiving, large maintenance

  Managed by anacron — missed jobs run at next boot. Drop any chmod +x
  script — no crontab syntax needed. Test with: run-parts --test DIR
OpsDeploy Q17 of 200
You need to run a one-time database migration script tonight at 11:00 PM, without creating a recurring cron job. Which tool is designed for one-time scheduled tasks?
A) sleep 3600 && /opt/anihpj/migrate.sh — calculate the seconds and sleep is the simplest approach
B) crontab -e with a single entry, then crontab -r after it runs
C) at 11:00 PM — the at command schedules a one-time job that runs once and is then removed automatically
D) systemd-run --on-calendar=23:00 /opt/anihpj/migrate.sh — systemd's transient timer is the modern approach
✅ Correct Answer: C

at is the one-time task scheduler. Unlike cron (recurring), at runs the command ONCE at the specified time and then removes itself. Use atq to list pending jobs and atrm to cancel.

💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ echo "/opt/anihpj/scripts/migrate.sh >> /var/log/anihpj/migrate.log 2>&1" | at 11:00 PM
warning: commands will be executed using /bin/sh
job 5 at Fri Jun 13 23:00:00 2026

$ atq
5 Fri Jun 13 23:00:00 2026 a anihpj

$ atrm 5
# Cancels job #5 if you change your mind

Use at for one-time database migrations, scheduled maintenance windows, or deploying at a specific time. The job runs in a minimal environment — use absolute paths and redirect output to a log file. This is useful for any application stack, regardless of language.

📝 at Command Quick Reference
at — One-Time Task Scheduler
  COMMAND                          EFFECT
  ───────────────────────────────  ──────────────────────────────────
  at 2:30 AM                       Schedule for specific time today
  at 11:00 PM tomorrow             Schedule for tomorrow
  at now + 2 hours                 Schedule relative to now
  echo "cmd" | at 23:00            Pipe command directly to at
  at -f script.sh 01:00            Schedule a script file
  atq                              List pending at jobs
  atrm 5                           Remove job #5

  Jobs run once, then auto-delete. Use absolute paths. Redirect output
  to log files — stdout is emailed to user by default (mail must be set up)
OpsDeploy Q18 of 200
What is the key advantage of systemd timers over traditional cron jobs?
A) systemd timers run faster because they bypass the shell entirely
B) systemd timers integrate with journald for centralized logging, support randomized delay windows, and can be listed with systemctl list-timers for a unified view of ALL scheduled tasks
C) systemd timers replace the need for anacron by running missed jobs automatically
D) systemd timers are the ONLY way to run scheduled tasks on modern Linux — cron is deprecated
✅ Correct Answer: B

Systemd timers offer: unified listing (systemctl list-timers shows all timers, next run, and last run), journald integration (every execution logged), randomized delays (RandomizedDelaySec prevents thundering herds), and monotonic timers (OnBootSec, OnUnitActiveSec — not dependent on wall clock).

💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ systemctl list-timers
NEXT LEFT LAST PASSED UNIT
Fri 2026-06-13 02:00:00 UTC 9h left Thu 2026-06-12 02:00:01 UTC 14h ago anihpj-backup.timer
Fri 2026-06-13 02:05:00 UTC 9h left Thu 2026-06-12 02:05:01 UTC 14h ago anihpj-cleanup.timer
Fri 2026-06-13 14:35:12 UTC 5min left Fri 2026-06-13 14:30:12 UTC 1s ago anihpj-health.timer

systemd timers are ideal for service-aware scheduling in any production environment. A backup timer can require postgresql.service (or mysql.service) to be running before executing, ensuring you never back up a stopped database. Cron has no concept of service dependencies. This is a universal Linux pattern — it works for Python, Node.js, Java, Go, Ruby, or any other stack.

📝 systemd Timers vs cron Comparison
systemd Timer = .timer file + .service file
  TIMER FILE                                    SERVICE FILE
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────  ────────────────────
  [Timer]                                       [Service]
  OnCalendar=daily                              ExecStart=/opt/anihpj/
  Persistent=true  ← run missed jobs             scripts/backup.sh
  RandomizedDelaySec=300  ← ±5 min spread        User=anihpj
  [Install]                                     [Install]
  WantedBy=timers.target                        WantedBy=...

  CRON vs SYSTEMD TIMERS:
  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ FEATURE       │ CRON            │ SYSTEMD TIMER                │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Listing       │ crontab -l (x N)│ systemctl list-timers (ALL)  │
  │ Logging       │ syslog only     │ journalctl + syslog           │
  │ Dependencies  │ None            │ Requires=/After= other units  │
  │ Random delay  │ sleep $RANDOM   │ RandomizedDelaySec=n          │
  │ Missed jobs   │ None (lost)     │ Persistent=true (catch-up)    │
  │ Monotonic     │ No              │ OnBootSec, OnUnitActiveSec    │
  └──────────────┴─────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘
OpsDeploy Q19 of 200
You suspect the nightly anihpj backup didn't run. How do you verify whether a cron job actually executed?
A) Check if the backup file exists — if it's there, the job ran
B) crontab -l — if the entry is listed, it definitely executed
C) systemctl status cron — cron service being active means all jobs ran
D) grep CRON /var/log/syslog — cron logs EVERY execution to syslog with timestamp, user, and command; also check your script's own log file if you added output redirection
✅ Correct Answer: D

Cron logs every execution to syslog. grep CRON /var/log/syslog shows the timestamp, username, and command. For systemd timers, use journalctl -u anihpj-backup.service. Always add >> /var/log/script.log 2>&1 to your cron commands for dedicated log files.

💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ grep CRON /var/log/syslog | grep backup | tail -3
Jun 13 02:00:01 anihpj CRON[8901]: (anihpj) CMD (/opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh)
Jun 12 02:00:01 anihpj CRON[7654]: (anihpj) CMD (/opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh)
Jun 11 02:00:01 anihpj CRON[6402]: (anihpj) CMD (/opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh)
# Executed every day at 2:00 AM ✅

Always add >> /var/log/anihpj/backup.log 2>&1 to cron commands. Your script should also log explicitly: echo "$(date): Backup completed successfully". Dual logging (syslog + script log) gives you both proof of execution AND the output — a universal best practice for any application stack.

📝 Verifying Cron & Timer Execution
How to Verify Scheduled Tasks Actually Ran
  CHECK                   COMMAND                                TELLS YOU
  ─────────────────────── ─────────────────────────────────────  ──────────
  Syslog for cron         grep CRON /var/log/syslog              Executed?
  Journal for timers      journalctl -u anihpj-backup.service    Executed?
  Your script log         tail /var/log/anihpj/backup.log        Succeeded?
  Pending at jobs         atq                                    What's next?
  All timers              systemctl list-timers --all            All + last
  Timer enable status     systemctl is-enabled anihpj-*.timer    Active?

  BEST PRACTICE: Always log both ways — syslog proves the job RAN,
  your script log proves it SUCCEEDED (with timestamps and exit codes).
  Monitor BOTH in production.
OpsDeploy Q20 of 200
Which combination of scheduled tasks is most appropriate for a production web application server?
A) One daily cron job that does everything: backup, cleanup, health check, and log rotation — simpler is better
B) No scheduled tasks — modern applications should be stateless and self-healing without cron
C) Multiple focused jobs: Daily PostgreSQL backup at 2AM (cron), health check every 5 min (systemd timer), log rotation weekly (logrotate), temp file cleanup daily (cron.daily), and SSL cert renewal check monthly (systemd timer)
D) All tasks as systemd timers — cron should never be used alongside systemd
✅ Correct Answer: C

A production server needs multiple independent scheduled tasks, each with different frequencies and purposes. Mixing cron, systemd timers, and logrotate is perfectly fine — each tool has strengths. cron is simple and universal, systemd timers offer better logging and dependencies, and logrotate is purpose-built for log management.

💻 Live Shell — Complete anihpj Schedule
$ crontab -l
0 2 * * * /opt/anihpj/scripts/backup.sh >> /var/log/anihpj/backup.log 2>&1

$ systemctl list-timers | grep anihpj
Fri 14:35:12 anihpj-health.timer anihpj-health.service
Sat 02:00:00 anihpj-backup.timer anihpj-backup.service

$ ls /etc/cron.daily/
anihpj-cleanup logrotate man-db

$ cat /etc/logrotate.d/anihpj
/var/log/anihpj/*.log { daily; rotate 30; compress; }

This hybrid approach gives any production server comprehensive automation: daily backups, continuous health monitoring, automatic log rotation, and regular cleanup — all independently scheduled and independently verifiable. Whether your app runs on Python, Node.js, Java, Go, or Ruby, this scheduling pattern is the gold standard for production readiness.

📝 Production Scheduling — The Hybrid Approach
The Complete Production Scheduling Stack
  FREQUENCY    TOOL              TASK
  ───────────  ────────────────  ──────────────────────────────────────────
  Every 5 min  systemd timer     Health check (curl /api/health)
  Daily 2AM    cron              PostgreSQL pg_dump backup
  Daily        cron.daily        Temp file cleanup (find -mtime +7 -delete)
  Weekly       logrotate         Rotate + compress application logs
  Monthly      systemd timer     SSL certificate renewal check

  HYBRID APPROACH = use the right tool for each job:
  • cron:       Simple, universal, perfect for daily backups
  • systemd timer: Logging (journald), dependencies, random delays
  • cron.daily:  Zero-syntax directory drops for cleanup scripts
  • logrotate:   Purpose-built for log management (compress, rotate, age)

  MONITOR ALL: grep CRON /var/log/syslog (daily) +
               systemctl list-timers --all +
               ls -la /var/log/anihpj/ (check backup logs exist)

1.4 Software Packages & RepositoriesQ21-Q26

OpsDeploy Q21 of 200
You need to install Nginx, PostgreSQL, and Redis on a fresh Ubuntu server for a production web stack. Which single apt command accomplishes this?
A) apt install nginx && apt install postgresql && apt install redis
B) apt-get install nginx postgresql redis — apt-get is the only supported package manager
C) sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y nginx postgresql redis — updates the package index first, then installs all three packages with automatic yes
D) snap install nginx postgresql redis — snap packages are the recommended method on Ubuntu
✅ Correct Answer: C

apt update refreshes the package index (you MUST do this first on a fresh system or you'll get 404 errors). apt install -y installs multiple packages in one command with automatic confirmation — essential for scripting. apt (not apt-get) is the modern, user-friendly frontend with progress bars and color output.

💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ sudo apt update
Hit:1 http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu jammy InRelease
Reading package lists... Done
Building dependency tree... Done
16 packages can be upgraded. Run 'apt list --upgradable' to see them.
$ sudo apt install -y nginx postgresql redis
Reading package lists... Done
The following NEW packages will be installed:
nginx nginx-common postgresql postgresql-15 redis redis-server ...
Setting up nginx (1.24.0-1ubuntu1) ...
Setting up postgresql-15 (15.3-1) ...
Setting up redis-server (7.0.8-1) ...
# All 3 packages installed ✅
$ apt list --installed | grep -E "nginx|postgresql|redis"
nginx/jammy,now 1.24.0-1ubuntu1 amd64 [installed]
postgresql/jammy,now 15.3-1 amd64 [installed]
redis/jammy,now 7.0.8-1 amd64 [installed]

The complete server bootstrap is sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y nginx postgresql redis plus your language runtime (python3-venv, nodejs, openjdk-21-jdk, golang, etc.). Always apt update before installing — without it, you may get stale package versions or 404 errors.

📝 apt vs apt-get vs dpkg Reference
Debian/Ubuntu Package Management — Three Layers
  LAYER           COMMAND        PURPOSE
  ─────────────── ────────────── ──────────────────────────────────────────
  HIGH (modern)   apt            User-friendly CLI with progress bars & color
  MID (stable)    apt-get/apt-cache  Scriptable; older, stable interface
  LOW (raw)       dpkg           Direct .deb operations, file ownership

  FREQUENT OPERATIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ COMMAND                       │ EFFECT                                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ apt update                    │ Refresh package index (RUN FIRST!)      │
  │ apt install -y PKG1 PKG2      │ Install multiple packages (auto-yes)   │
  │ apt upgrade                   │ Upgrade all installed packages          │
  │ apt-mark hold/unhold PKG      │ Prevent/allow package upgrades          │
  │ dpkg -S /path/to/file         │ Which package owns this file?           │
  │ dpkg -L nginx                 │ List ALL files installed by package     │
  │ add-apt-repository ppa:X/Y    │ Add a PPA repository                    │
  │ apt list --installed           │ List all installed packages             │
  └──────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘
OpsDeploy Q22 of 200
A configuration file has been modified on the anihpj server. You need to know which installed package owns the file /usr/bin/python3. Which command answers this?
A) apt show /usr/bin/python3 — apt can reverse-lookup file ownership
B) dpkg -S /usr/bin/python3 — searches the dpkg database for which package installed this file
C) which python3 — shows the path and the owning package
D) file /usr/bin/python3 — the file type command includes package metadata
✅ Correct Answer: B

dpkg -S (--search) queries the dpkg database for which package owns a file. It works for any file that was installed by a Debian package. dpkg -L nginx does the reverse — lists all files installed by a package.

💻 Live Shell — See It In Action
$ dpkg -S /usr/bin/python3
python3-minimal: /usr/bin/python3

$ dpkg -S /etc/nginx/nginx.conf
nginx-common: /etc/nginx/nginx.conf

$ dpkg -L nginx | head -5
/usr/sbin/nginx
/usr/share/nginx
/etc/nginx
# Reverse: all files from the nginx package

Use dpkg -S to identify which package owns a modified config file before upgrading. Use dpkg --verify nginx to check if any installed files have been modified from their original versions. These dpkg skills apply to any application stack, regardless of language.

📝 dpkg — Direct Package Operations
dpkg S vs L vs --verify — Three Ways to Inspect Packages
  COMMAND                   DIRECTION        TELLS YOU
  ───────────────────────── ───────────────  ────────────────────────────
  dpkg -S /path/to/file     File → Package   "Who owns this file?"
  dpkg -L nginx             Package → Files  "What files did nginx install?"
  dpkg --verify nginx       Compare → Diff   "Have any files been modified?"

  EXAMPLE:
  $ dpkg --verify nginx
  ??5??????   c /etc/nginx/nginx.conf
  # md5 mismatch — config file was edited (expected for configs)

  $ dpkg --verify python3
  (no output = all files match original checksums — pristine)
OpsDeploy Q23 of 200
Where does Ubuntu store its list of package repositories, and how do you add a PPA for a newer Python version?
A) /etc/apt/sources.list + /etc/apt/sources.list.d/*.list; add PPA with sudo add-apt-repository ppa:deadsnakes/ppa
B) /var/lib/apt/lists/ — this directory directly stores repository URLs
C) ~/.apt/sources — each user has their own repository configuration
D) /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/ — repositories are configured as apt configuration directives
✅ Correct Answer: A

/etc/apt/sources.list is the main repository file. /etc/apt/sources.list.d/ contains additional .list files (one per repository — easier to manage). add-apt-repository automates adding PPAs by creating the .list file and importing the GPG key.

💻 Live Shell
$ sudo add-apt-repository ppa:deadsnakes/ppa
Repository: 'deb https://ppa.launchpadcontent.net/deadsnakes/ppa/ubuntu jammy main'
Adding repository.

$ sudo apt update && sudo apt install python3.12
📝 APT Repository Locations
Where Ubuntu Looks for Packages
  /etc/apt/sources.list              ← Main repo file (can edit directly)
  /etc/apt/sources.list.d/*.list     ← One .list file per repo (PREFERRED)

  TYPICAL sources.list LINE:
  deb http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu jammy main restricted universe multiverse
   │   │                               │     │
   │   └─ URI of repository            │     └─ Components (sections)
   └─ Type: deb (binary) / deb-src (source)
                                         └─ Suite/codename (jammy, noble...)

  ADDING A PPA:
  sudo add-apt-repository ppa:deadsnakes/ppa
  → Creates /etc/apt/sources.list.d/deadsnakes-ubuntu-ppa-jammy.list
  → Imports GPG signing key automatically
  → sudo apt update (required after!)
OpsDeploy Q24 of 200
A critical security patch for Nginx is available, but you're not ready to upgrade the anihpj production server yet. How do you prevent Nginx from being accidentally upgraded while still allowing other packages to update?
A) Remove the Nginx repository from sources.list — no repository means no upgrades
B) apt remove nginx then apt install nginx=1.24.0 — pin to the current version
C) chmod -x /usr/sbin/nginx — prevent the binary from being replaced
D) sudo apt-mark hold nginx — marks the package as "held back" so apt upgrade skips it; use apt-mark unhold nginx when ready
✅ Correct Answer: D

apt-mark hold prevents a package from being upgraded, installed, or removed. The package stays at its current version until you apt-mark unhold it. apt-mark showhold lists all held packages.

💻 Live Shell
$ sudo apt-mark hold nginx
nginx set on hold.

$ sudo apt upgrade
The following packages have been kept back:
nginx

$ apt-mark showhold
nginx

$ sudo apt-mark unhold nginx
Canceled hold on nginx. ✅
📝 apt-mark — Package Hold/Pin Management
apt-mark — Control Package Upgrade Behavior
  COMMAND                      EFFECT
  ───────────────────────────  ──────────────────────────────────────────
  apt-mark hold PKG             Prevent PKG from being upgraded
  apt-mark unhold PKG           Allow PKG to be upgraded again
  apt-mark showhold             List all held packages
  apt-mark auto PKG             Mark as auto-installed (removed if unused)
  apt-mark manual PKG           Mark as manually installed (keep forever)
  apt-mark showmanual           List all manually installed packages

  USE CASE: Hold nginx before dist-upgrade, hold database server during
  maintenance window, hold a specific kernel version known to work.
  ⚠️  Holding security patches = risk — always have a plan to unhold!
OpsDeploy Q25 of 200
What is the key difference between snap packages and traditional apt/deb packages?
A) Snap packages are faster because they are compiled specifically for your CPU architecture
B) Snap packages are sandboxed, auto-updating, and bundle all dependencies — they run in isolation with restricted system access, while apt packages integrate with the system's shared libraries
C) Snap packages are only for desktop applications; apt is the only option for server software
D) Snap packages are installed in /opt/ while apt packages go in /usr/
✅ Correct Answer: B

Snaps are confined, auto-updating, bundled packages. They include all dependencies (no dependency hell), update automatically in the background, and run with restricted system access via AppArmor. Apt packages share system libraries (smaller, but can conflict).

💻 Live Shell
$ snap install certbot --classic
certbot 2.9.0 from Certbot Project (certbot-eff) installed

$ snap list
Name Version Rev Tracking Publisher Notes
certbot 2.9.0 342 latest/stable certbot-eff classic
lxd 5.20 262 latest/stable canonical -
📝 Snap vs Apt — Side-by-Side Comparison
Snap Packages vs Traditional Apt/deb Packages
  ┌─────────────────┬──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ FEATURE          │ SNAP                 │ APT/DEB                  │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Dependencies     │ Bundled (all-in-one) │ Shared system libraries  │
  │ Disk size        │ Larger (bundles deps)│ Smaller (shares libs)    │
  │ Updates          │ Auto, background     │ Manual (apt upgrade)     │
  │ Sandboxing       │ Yes (AppArmor)       │ No (full system access)  │
  │ Install location │ /snap/               │ /usr/, /etc/, /var/      │
  │ Rollback         │ snap revert          │ apt install PKG=version  │
  │ Channels         │ stable/candidate/beta/edge │ Repo-based only     │
  │ Best for         │ Desktop apps, certbot│ Server daemons, libs     │
  └─────────────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘
OpsDeploy Q26 of 200
For a web application, language-specific dependencies (Python packages, Node modules, Java JARs) are managed outside of apt. What is the recommended approach for installing application dependencies on a production server?
A) sudo pip install -r requirements.txt — pip with sudo installs packages system-wide (applies to npm -g, gem install too)
B) apt install python3-django python3-psycopg2 — only use apt/distro packages for all dependencies
C) python3 -m venv /opt/anihpj/venv && /opt/anihpj/venv/bin/pip install -r requirements.txt — create an isolated virtual environment and install dependencies there, never as root. Equivalent patterns: npm ci (Node.js), mvn install (Java), go mod download (Go), bundle install --deployment (Ruby)
D) conda install --file requirements.txt — Conda is the standard Python package manager for production
✅ Correct Answer: C

Language-specific dependency isolation (venv for Python, node_modules for Node.js, Maven/Gradle caches for Java, Go modules) keeps dependencies per application. Never use sudo with pip, npm, or gem — it conflicts with apt-managed packages and can break system tools. The isolation keeps your app's dependencies completely separate from the system. This is a universal best practice across ALL languages.

💻 Live Shell
$ sudo apt install -y python3-venv python3-pip

$ python3 -m venv /opt/anihpj/venv

$ /opt/anihpj/venv/bin/pip install -r /opt/anihpj/requirements.txt
Successfully installed Django-5.0.6 gunicorn-21.2.0 psycopg2-2.9.9 ...
📝 Language Dependency Isolation — Universal Pattern
Per-Language Isolation — Never Use sudo with App Package Managers
  LANGUAGE    ISOLATION TOOL       INSTALL COMMAND (per-app, NOT system)
  ─────────── ──────────────────── ────────────────────────────────────────
  Python      venv / virtualenv    python3 -m venv /opt/app/venv
                                   /opt/app/venv/bin/pip install -r req.txt
  Node.js     node_modules (local)  npm ci  (or npm install, no -g!)
  Java        Maven/Gradle cache    mvn install  (local ~/.m2 cache)
  Go          Go modules            go mod download  (per-module cache)
  Ruby        Bundler               bundle install --deployment --path vendor
  Rust        Cargo                 cargo build --release (per-project)

  ⚠️  NEVER: sudo pip install, sudo npm install -g (for app deps),
             sudo gem install — these fight with apt and break system tools!
  ✅  ALWAYS: Isolate dependencies per application in its own directory.

1.5 System Recovery — Boot, fsck & BackupsQ27-Q32

OpsDeploy Q27 of 200
The anihpj production server fails to boot after a kernel update and drops to the GRUB rescue prompt. What is the first step to recover the system?
A) Reinstall Ubuntu from ISO — a failed boot means the OS is corrupted beyond repair
B) At the GRUB menu, select an older kernel from "Advanced options" — the previous working kernel is still installed and can boot the system normally
C) Type exit at the GRUB rescue prompt — this automatically loads the last known good configuration
D) Run fsck /dev/sda from the GRUB prompt — filesystem corruption is always the cause of boot failure
✅ Correct Answer: B

Ubuntu keeps the last 2-3 kernels installed. When a new kernel causes boot failure, select an older kernel from "Advanced options for Ubuntu" in the GRUB menu. The system boots normally with the old kernel. Then you can investigate why the new kernel failed and decide whether to remove it.

💻 Live Shell
$ dpkg --list | grep linux-image
ii linux-image-6.8.0-31-generic 6.8.0-31.31 amd64 Linux kernel image
ii linux-image-6.8.0-30-generic 6.8.0-30.30 amd64 Linux kernel image
# Two kernels installed — if -31 fails, boot -30 from GRUB
📝 GRUB Boot Recovery Flow
What To Do When the Server Won't Boot
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ BOOT FAILURE RECOVERY DECISION TREE                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │ Server won't boot?                                               │
  │   ├─ GRUB menu still appears?                                    │
  │   │   └─ "Advanced options" → Select OLDER kernel → Boot ✅      │
  │   │      Then: dpkg --list | grep linux-image (see all kernels)  │
  │   │      Then: sudo apt remove linux-image-BAD-VERSION           │
  │   │                                                               │
  │   ├─ GRUB rescue prompt? (grub>)                                 │
  │   │   └─ ls (find partition), set root=..., linux /vmlinuz...    │
  │   │      initrd /initrd..., boot                                  │
  │   │                                                               │
  │   └─ No GRUB at all? → Boot from Live USB → chroot → update-grub │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
OpsDeploy Q28 of 200
You've lost the root password on the anihpj server and cannot log in. How do you reset the root password with physical access to the machine?
A) Boot to GRUB → edit kernel line → add rw init=/bin/bash → boot → mount -o remount,rw / → passwd → reboot
B) Boot from Ubuntu Live USB → mount the disk → edit /etc/shadow and delete the root password hash
C) Press Ctrl+Alt+F2 at the login screen to access a root shell without a password
D) sudo passwd root from any user account — sudo doesn't require the root password
✅ Correct Answer: A

Adding init=/bin/bash to the kernel command line skips the normal init process (systemd) and drops directly into a root shell. The rw parameter mounts the root filesystem as read-write (default is read-only in recovery). This works because physical access = root access — which is why production servers should use full disk encryption (LUKS).

💻 Live Shell
# mount -o remount,rw /
# passwd
New password: ********
Retype new password: ********
passwd: password updated successfully
# reboot -f
📝 Root Password Reset — The init=/bin/bash Method
Resetting Root Password via GRUB (Physical Access Required)
  STEP 1: At GRUB menu, press 'e' on the kernel line
  STEP 2: Find line starting with "linux /boot/vmlinuz..."
  STEP 3: Add: rw init=/bin/bash  (at end of that line)
  STEP 4: Press Ctrl+X or F10 to boot
  STEP 5: You are now root in a bash shell:
          # mount -o remount,rw /      (if not rw)
          # passwd                      (set new root password)
          # reboot -f                   (force reboot)

  ⚠️  Physical access = root access on unencrypted systems.
      PROTECT: Use LUKS full-disk encryption on production servers.
      For cloud VMs: Use cloud provider's console + recovery ISO instead.
OpsDeploy Q29 of 200
After an unexpected power loss, the anihpj server's PostgreSQL data volume fails to mount with "unclean shutdown" errors. Which tool repairs the filesystem?
A) systemctl reset-failed — systemd can repair filesystem mount failures
B) tune2fs -L anihpj-db /dev/sdb1 — relabeling the volume resets the dirty flag
C) mkfs.ext4 /dev/sdb1 — reformatting is the only way to fix corruption
D) fsck /dev/sdb1 (filesystem UNMOUNTED) — checks and repairs the filesystem journal, replaying any incomplete transactions from the power loss
✅ Correct Answer: D

fsck (File System Consistency checK) replays the filesystem journal to recover from unclean shutdowns. The filesystem MUST be unmounted before running fsck. Journaling filesystems (ext4, XFS) recover quickly because they only replay recent transactions, not scan the entire disk.

💻 Live Shell
$ sudo umount /var/lib/postgresql

$ sudo fsck -y /dev/anihpj-vg/anihpj-db
fsck from util-linux 2.37.2
anihpj-db: recovering journal
anihpj-db: clean, 234567/6553600 files, 12456789/26214400 blocks

$ sudo mount /var/lib/postgresql
# Filesystem repaired and remounted ✅
📝 fsck — Filesystem Repair Reference
fsck — When and How to Repair Filesystems
  COMMAND                    WHEN TO USE
  ─────────────────────────  ────────────────────────────────────────────
  fsck -y /dev/sdb1          Auto-repair ALL errors (-y = yes to all)
  fsck -n /dev/sdb1          Dry-run: check only, don't fix
  fsck -f /dev/sdb1          Force check even if filesystem looks clean

  FILESYSTEM MUST BE UNMOUNTED (or mounted read-only).

  FILESYSTEM TYPES:
  ┌──────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ext4     │ fsck.ext4 (journal replay — fast recovery)               │
  │ XFS      │ xfs_repair (NO fsck — XFS has its own tool)              │
  │ Btrfs    │ btrfs check --repair (CoW — different recovery model)    │
  │ ZFS      │ zpool scrub (self-healing — no fsck needed)              │
  └──────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️  NEVER run fsck on a mounted filesystem — it WILL corrupt data!
      For root fs: boot from Live USB, or use tune2fs to force check
      on next reboot: sudo tune2fs -c 1 /dev/sda1
OpsDeploy Q30 of 200
You accidentally deleted /var/log/anihpj/app.log, but df still shows the disk as full. Why?
A) df caches results — run df --refresh to update
B) A running process (the app server) still has the file open — the file is deleted from the directory but the data blocks are NOT freed until the process closes the file descriptor
C) Deleted files are moved to a trash directory — you need to empty ~/.local/share/Trash/
D) df shows allocated space, not used space — the numbers don't change when files are deleted
✅ Correct Answer: B

In Linux, a file is only truly deleted when all file descriptors referencing it are closed. If the app server process has the log file open and writes to it, deleting the directory entry doesn't free the disk space. lsof | grep deleted finds these "ghost" files — this is a universal Linux behavior that applies regardless of your application's language.

💻 Live Shell
$ sudo lsof | grep deleted | grep app
app-srv 1234 anihpj 5w REG 253,0 2147483648 12345 /var/log/anihpj/app.log (deleted)

$ sudo systemctl restart anihpj-api
# After restart, the file is truly deleted and disk space is freed ✅
📝 Ghost Files — Deleted But Still Using Disk
Why "rm file.log" Doesn't Always Free Space
  LINUX FILE DELETION — TWO-STEP PROCESS:

  ┌─────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────┐
  │ Directory Entry  │────▶│ Inode                │────▶│ Data Blocks     │
  │ (filename)       │     │ (metadata, refcount)  │     │ (actual content)│
  └─────────────────┘     └──────────────────────┘     └─────────────────┘
       rm removes             refcount > 0 →               Freed only when
       THIS link only         inode NOT freed              refcount = 0

  FINDING GHOST FILES:
  $ sudo lsof | grep deleted          # List all open-but-deleted files
  $ sudo lsof +L1                      # Same, shorter syntax

  FIX OPTIONS:
  1. Restart the process holding the file (cleanest)
  2. > /proc/PID/fd/FD_NUM            (truncate the file via /proc)
  3. kill -HUP PID                     (some daemons reopen logs on HUP)
OpsDeploy Q31 of 200
After a kernel panic, you need to see the boot messages from the PREVIOUS boot to understand what caused the crash. Which command shows logs from the last boot?
A) dmesg — shows the current kernel ring buffer, which persists across reboots
B) cat /var/log/boot.log — the boot log is always archived on panic
C) journalctl -b -1 — shows all logs from the PREVIOUS boot (-1); -b 0 is current, -b -2 is two boots ago
D) systemd-analyze blame --previous — analyzes the previous boot's timing
✅ Correct Answer: C

journalctl -b -1 accesses systemd's persistent journal to show logs from the previous boot. This only works if the journal is stored on disk (/var/log/journal/ exists). dmesg only shows the CURRENT boot's kernel ring buffer — it's cleared on reboot.

💻 Live Shell
$ journalctl -b -1 | grep -i "error\|panic\|oom"
kernel: Out of memory: Killed process 1234 (app-server)
kernel: oom-kill:constraint=CONSTRAINT_NONE

$ journalctl --list-boots
-2 abc123... Wed 2026-06-11 08:00:00 — Wed 2026-06-11 20:00:00
-1 def456... Thu 2026-06-12 08:00:00 — Thu 2026-06-12 14:30:52
0 ghi789... Thu 2026-06-12 14:35:00 — Fri 2026-06-13 09:00:00
📝 journalctl Boot Navigation
Navigating Logs Across Reboots
  COMMAND                        SHOWS
  ─────────────────────────────  ──────────────────────────────────────
  journalctl -b 0                 Current boot (default)
  journalctl -b -1                Previous boot (crashed? look here!)
  journalctl -b -2                Two boots ago
  journalctl --list-boots         List all boots with timestamps
  journalctl -b -1 -p err         Previous boot — errors only
  journalctl -b -1 -k             Previous boot — kernel messages only

  REQUIRES: /var/log/journal/ exists (persistent journal storage).
  If only /run/log/journal/ exists → volatile, lost on reboot.

  ENABLE PERSISTENT JOURNAL:
  $ sudo mkdir -p /var/log/journal
  $ sudo systemd-tmpfiles --create --prefix /var/log/journal
  $ sudo systemctl restart systemd-journald
OpsDeploy Q32 of 200
What is the recommended backup strategy for a production web application server?
A) Daily rsync of /opt/anihpj/ to an external drive — application code is the most critical data
B) LVM snapshot once a week — block-level backup captures everything in one operation
C) tar czf /backup/anihpj-$(date +%F).tar.gz /opt/anihpj/ — compressed tarball of the entire app
D) Layered strategy: pg_dump for PostgreSQL (daily), rsync for media/uploads (daily), tar for /etc/ configs (weekly), all stored off-server with retention policies
✅ Correct Answer: D

Different data types need different backup strategies. PostgreSQL needs pg_dump (logical backup that's consistent at the transaction level). User uploads need rsync (efficient incremental transfer). Configs need version control (git) plus periodic tar. ALL backups must be stored off-server.

💻 Live Shell
$ sudo -u postgres pg_dump anihpj_db | gzip > /backup/db-$(date +%F).sql.gz
$ rsync -avz /opt/anihpj/media/ backup-server:/backups/anihpj-media/
$ tar czf /backup/configs-$(date +%F).tar.gz /etc/anihpj/ /etc/nginx/ /etc/postgresql/

For any production server: Schedule these in cron (daily at 2AM), verify backups monthly by restoring to a test VM, and monitor backup logs for failures. Untested backups are not backups. This layered strategy works for Python, Node.js, Java, Go, Ruby, or any other stack — only the database dump command changes (pg_dump vs mysqldump vs mongodump).

📝 Backup Strategy — Language-Agnostic Layers
The 3-Layer Production Backup Strategy
  LAYER 1: DATABASE (daily, logical dump)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ PostgreSQL: pg_dump DB | gzip > db-$(date +%F).sql.gz          │
  │ MySQL:      mysqldump DB | gzip > db-$(date +%F).sql.gz        │
  │ MongoDB:    mongodump --archive --gzip > db-$(date +%F).gz     │
  │ SQLite:     sqlite3 db.sqlite3 .dump | gzip > db-$(date +%F).gz│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  LAYER 2: USER DATA / MEDIA (daily, incremental sync)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ rsync -avz --delete /opt/app/media/ backup-host:/backups/media/ │
  │ rsync -avz --delete /opt/app/uploads/ backup-host:/backups/up/  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  LAYER 3: CONFIGURATION (weekly + git version control)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ tar czf /backup/configs-$(date +%F).tar.gz /etc/app/ /etc/nginx/│
  │ AND: /etc/ in git repo (etckeeper or manual)                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  GOLDEN RULES:
  1. ALL backups stored OFF-SERVER (different physical/cloud host)
  2. Retention: Daily×7, Weekly×4, Monthly×12 — automate rotation
  3. TEST RESTORES monthly — untested backups are NOT backups
  4. Monitor: grep "backup" /var/log/syslog daily or set up alerting

1.6 Virtual Machines — libvirt & KVMQ33-Q38

OpsDeploy Q33 of 200
What is the libvirt architecture and how does it relate to KVM?
A) libvirt replaces KVM — it's a newer, faster hypervisor that doesn't need hardware virtualization
B) libvirt is a management API/daemon (libvirtd) that provides a unified interface to manage VMs across different hypervisors; KVM is the actual hypervisor (kernel module) that performs hardware-accelerated virtualization via /dev/kvm
C) libvirt and KVM are the same thing — the terms are interchangeable
D) libvirt is the GUI (virt-manager) and KVM is the CLI (virsh)
✅ Correct Answer: B

libvirt is the management layer — it provides a consistent API (virsh CLI, virt-manager GUI, libvirtd daemon) that works with KVM, QEMU, Xen, and LXC. KVM is the kernel module that turns Linux into a hypervisor using hardware virtualization extensions (Intel VT-x/AMD-V).

💻 Live Shell
$ sudo apt install -y qemu-kvm libvirt-daemon-system virtinst

$ sudo systemctl status libvirtd
● libvirtd.service - Virtualization daemon
Active: active (running)

$ ls -l /dev/kvm
crw-rw---- 1 root kvm 10, 232 Jun 13 09:00 /dev/kvm

How libvirt, KVM, QEMU, and virsh Fit Together
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  USER SPACE                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐                              │
  │  │  virt-manager │  │    virsh     │  ← Management Tools          │
  │  │  (GUI)        │  │  (CLI shell) │                              │
  │  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘                              │
  │         │                 │                                       │
  │         └────────┬────────┘                                       │
  │                  ▼                                                │
  │         ┌────────────────┐                                       │
  │         │   libvirtd     │  ← Management Daemon (API)             │
  │         └───────┬────────┘                                       │
  │                 │                                                 │
  │                 ▼                                                 │
  │         ┌────────────────┐                                       │
  │         │  QEMU          │  ← Hardware Emulation                  │
  │         └───────┬────────┘                                       │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  KERNEL SPACE   ▼                                                │
  │         ┌────────────────┐                                       │
  │         │  KVM (kvm.ko)  │  ← Hypervisor (VT-x / AMD-V)          │
  │         │  /dev/kvm      │                                       │
  │         └────────────────┘                                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  VERIFY: ls -l /dev/kvm  (if missing → CPU doesn't support virtualization
                           or it's disabled in BIOS)
          egrep -c '(vmx|svm)' /proc/cpuinfo  (should be >0)
OpsDeploy Q34 of 200
You need to list all VMs on the anihpj virtualization host and start a VM named "anihpj-staging". Which virsh commands do this?
A) virsh list --all to see all VMs (running + stopped), then virsh start anihpj-staging
B) virsh show to list, then virsh boot anihpj-staging to start
C) systemctl list-units kvm-* — VMs are managed as systemd units
D) qemu-system-x86_64 -list — QEMU directly manages the VM registry
✅ Correct Answer: A

virsh list shows only RUNNING VMs. virsh list --all shows ALL VMs including shut-off ones. virsh start boots a VM, virsh shutdown sends ACPI shutdown, virsh destroy force-stops (like pulling the power cord).

1 anihpj-prod running
- anihpj-staging shut off

$ virsh start anihpj-staging

📝 virsh — Essential VM Management Commands
virsh Quick Reference — Manage VMs Like a Pro
  COMMAND                                    EFFECT
  ─────────────────────────────────────────  ──────────────────────────────
  virsh list                                  Running VMs only
  virsh list --all                            ALL VMs (running + stopped)
  virsh start VM                              Boot a VM
  virsh shutdown VM                           Graceful ACPI shutdown
  virsh destroy VM                            Force-stop (pull power cord)
  virsh reboot VM                             Reboot a running VM
  virsh autostart VM                          Auto-start at host boot
  virsh autostart --disable VM                Disable auto-start
  virsh dominfo VM                            Detailed VM info (RAM, CPU...)
  virsh domstats VM                           Live resource statistics
  virsh undefine VM                           Remove VM config (keep disk)
  virsh undefine VM --remove-all-storage      Remove VM + DELETE disk!
  virsh console VM                            Connect to VM's serial console

B) qemu-img create anihpj-test.qcow2 20G && qemu-system-x86_64 -m 2048 -smp 2

OpsDeploy Q36 of 200
What is the difference between NAT and bridged networking for KVM virtual machines?
A) NAT is faster; bridged is more secure — choose based on your priority
B) NAT: VMs are on a private network (192.168.122.0/24), access external networks through the host (masquerading), not directly reachable from outside. Bridged: VMs appear as separate physical machines on the same network as the host, get IPs from the LAN's DHCP, and are directly reachable
C) NAT uses the default virtual network; bridged requires a separate physical NIC per VM
D) There is no practical difference — both provide identical connectivity
✅ Correct Answer: B

NAT (default) is simpler: VMs get 192.168.122.x addresses, outbound traffic is masqueraded through the host. Bridged mode uses a Linux bridge to connect VMs directly to the physical network — VMs get IPs from the LAN router and are reachable like physical servers.

💻 Live Shell
$ virsh net-list
Name State Autostart Persistent
default active yes yes

$ virsh net-dumpxml default | grep "ip address"
<ip address='192.168.122.1' netmask='255.255.255.0'>
📝 NAT vs Bridged Networking — Visualized
KVM Networking Modes — NAT (Default) vs Bridged
  NAT MODE (virbr0 — default):
  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐
  │  VM1         │  │  VM2         │
  │ 192.168.122.50│  │ 192.168.122.51│
  └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬──────┘
         │                 │
         └────────┬────────┘
                  ▼
         ┌────────────────┐
         │  virbr0 (NAT)  │  ← Host acts as router, masquerades traffic
         │  192.168.122.1  │
         └───────┬────────┘
                 │
         ┌───────┴────────┐
         │  HOST (eth0)    │  ← VMs NOT visible on LAN
         │  10.0.0.10      │     Only host-initiated connections work
         └────────────────┘

  BRIDGED MODE (br0):
  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐
  │  VM1         │  │  VM2         │  │  HOST           │
  │ 10.0.0.50    │  │ 10.0.0.51    │  │ 10.0.0.10       │
  └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬──────┘  └───────┬────────┘
         │                 │                 │
         └────────┬────────┴─────────────────┘
                  ▼
         ┌────────────────┐
         │  br0 (Bridge)   │  ← All devices on same L2 network
         └───────┬────────┘
                 ▼
         ┌────────────────┐
         │  Physical eth0  │  ← VMs appear as separate machines on LAN
         │  → LAN Router   │
         └────────────────┘
OpsDeploy Q37 of 200
Before a risky database migration on the anihpj-staging VM, you want to create a snapshot so you can roll back if it fails. Which virsh command does this?
A) virsh snapshot-create-as anihpj-staging --name "pre-migration" --description "Before DB schema v5"
B) cp /var/lib/libvirt/images/anihpj-staging.qcow2 /backup/ — manual disk copy is the only reliable snapshot method
C) virsh save anihpj-staging /backup/anihpj-staging.state — saves RAM state to a file
D) lvsnapshot create anihpj-staging /dev/vg/snapshot — LVM snapshots are preferred over virsh
✅ Correct Answer: A

virsh snapshot-create-as creates a named snapshot of the VM's disk state. The VM can be running or shut off. Rollback: virsh snapshot-revert anihpj-staging --snapshotname "pre-migration". Snapshots use qcow2's copy-on-write internally — only changed blocks consume additional space.

💻 Live Shell
$ virsh snapshot-create-as anihpj-staging --name "pre-migration"
Domain snapshot pre-migration created

$ virsh snapshot-list anihpj-staging
Name Creation Time State
pre-migration 2026-06-13 14:00:00 +0000 running

$ virsh snapshot-revert anihpj-staging --snapshotname "pre-migration"
📝 VM Snapshots — Safe Experimentation Workflow
Snapshot Lifecycle — Create, Test, Revert
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ SNAPSHOT WORKFLOW:                                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │ 1. CREATE (save state)                                           │
  │    $ virsh snapshot-create-as VM --name "before-risky-change"    │
  │                                                                  │
  │ 2. DO THE RISKY THING                                            │
  │    (migrate DB, upgrade OS, change config...)                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │ 3a. SUCCESS → DELETE snapshot (no longer needed)                 │
  │     $ virsh snapshot-delete VM --snapshotname "before-risky"     │
  │                                                                  │
  │ 3b. FAILURE → REVERT to snapshot                                 │
  │     $ virsh snapshot-revert VM --snapshotname "before-risky"     │
  │                                                                  │
  │ COMMANDS:                                                        │
  │ virsh snapshot-list VM                 List all snapshots        │
  │ virsh snapshot-info VM --snapshotname X  Show snapshot details   │
  │ virsh snapshot-current VM              Which snapshot is active  │
  │ virsh snapshot-delete VM --snapshotname X  Remove a snapshot     │
  │ virsh snapshot-delete VM --children    Delete snapshot + children│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

B) Create one VM and have all developers SSH into it — shared environments reduce inconsistency

1.7 Container Engines — Docker & PodmanQ39-Q44

OpsDeploy Q39 of 200
What is the fundamental difference between containers and virtual machines?
A) Containers are just lightweight VMs — there is no architectural difference
B) VMs: each has its own kernel, virtualized hardware, and full OS — heavy isolation. Containers: share the host kernel, use namespaces for isolation and cgroups for resource limits — lightweight, start in milliseconds
C) Containers require more RAM than VMs because of the container runtime overhead
D) VMs share the host kernel like containers but add hardware emulation for compatibility
✅ Correct Answer: B

VMs virtualize HARDWARE (each has its own kernel, drivers, init system). Containers virtualize the OS (share the host kernel, isolated via namespaces). Containers start in milliseconds (just a process), VMs take seconds (boot an entire OS).

💻 Live Shell
$ docker run --rm alpine echo "Hello from container"
Hello from container
# Started and finished in ~0.5 seconds

$ lsns -t pid
NS TYPE NPROCS PID USER COMMAND
4026531836 pid 130 1 root /sbin/init
4026532567 pid 2 5678 anihpj /usr/bin/gunicorn
📝 Containers vs VMs — Side-by-Side
Containers vs Virtual Machines — Architecture Comparison
  VIRTUAL MACHINES:                          CONTAINERS:
  ┌─────────────────────┐                    ┌─────────────────────┐
  │ App A    │ App B     │                    │ App A    │ App B     │
  │ Bins/Libs│ Bins/Libs │                    │ Bins/Libs│ Bins/Libs │
  │ Guest OS │ Guest OS  │                    ├─────────────────────┤
  ├──────────┴──────────┤                    │ Container Runtime    │
  │   Hypervisor (KVM)   │                    ├─────────────────────┤
  ├──────────────────────┤                    │     Host Kernel       │
  │    Host Kernel        │                    ├──────────────────────┤
  ├──────────────────────┤                    │    Hardware            │
  │    Hardware            │                    └─────────────────────┘
  └──────────────────────┘
  • Full OS per app       • GBs each          • Share kernel         • MBs each
  • Boot: seconds          • Strong isolation  • Start: milliseconds  • Weaker isolation
  • Own kernel, drivers    • Heavyweight       • Linux namespaces     • Lightweight
OpsDeploy Q40 of 200
You need to run the anihpj-api as a Docker container, exposing it on port 8000 and ensuring it restarts automatically. Which command does this?
A) docker run -d --name anihpj-api -p 8000:8000 --restart unless-stopped anihpj/anihpj-api:v2.1.0
B) docker start anihpj-api --port 8000 --restart always
C) docker deploy anihpj-api --port 8000:8000
D) docker run --foreground anihpj-api:latest -e PORT=8000
✅ Correct Answer: A

-d = detached (background), --name = assign a name, -p 8000:8000 = host:container port mapping, --restart unless-stopped = auto-restart on crash or Docker daemon restart (but not if manually stopped).

💻 Live Shell
$ docker run -d --name anihpj-api -p 8000:8000 --restart unless-stopped anihpj/anihpj-api:v2.1.0
a1b2c3d4e5f6...

$ docker ps
CONTAINER ID IMAGE STATUS PORTS
a1b2c3d4e5f6 anihpj/anihpj-api:v2.1.0 Up 5 seconds 0.0.0.0:8000->8000/tcp
📝 docker run — Essential Parameters
docker run — The Most Important Flags
  FLAG                        EFFECT
  ──────────────────────────  ────────────────────────────────────────────
  -d                          Detached (run in background, print container ID)
  --name NAME                  Assign a name (instead of random 2-word name)
  -p HOST:CONT                 Port mapping (e.g. -p 8000:8000)
  -p 127.0.0.1:8000:8000       Bind to localhost only (not public!)
  -v /host/path:/cont/path     Bind mount (persistent data)
  -v vol-name:/cont/path       Named volume (Docker-managed storage)
  -e KEY=VALUE                 Environment variable
  --restart always             Always restart (docker daemon restart too)
  --restart unless-stopped     Restart unless manually stopped (RECOMMENDED)
  --restart on-failure:5       Restart only on crash, max 5 times
  --rm                         Auto-remove container when it stops
  --network NET                Connect to a specific Docker network
  -u 1000                      Run as UID 1000 (non-root!)
OpsDeploy Q41 of 200
What is the key advantage of Podman over Docker for production Linux servers?
A) Podman is faster because it's written in Rust instead of Go
B) Podman is daemonless (no background service) and supports rootless containers — containers run as the user without root privileges, and podman generate systemd creates native systemd unit files for containers
C) Podman has a built-in Kubernetes API server for container orchestration
D) Podman uses a different image format that is more secure than OCI
✅ Correct Answer: B

Podman is daemonless — no long-running background process (unlike dockerd). It supports rootless mode (containers run as the user's UID, no root needed). podman generate systemd creates native systemd units, integrating containers into the standard Linux service management.

💻 Live Shell
$ podman run -d --name anihpj-api -p 8000:8000 anihpj/anihpj-api:v2.1.0

$ podman generate systemd --name anihpj-api > ~/.config/systemd/user/anihpj-api.service

$ systemctl --user enable --now anihpj-api
# Container now managed as a native systemd service! ✅
📝 Docker vs Podman — Comparison
Docker vs Podman — Key Differences
  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │ FEATURE       │ DOCKER               │ PODMAN                         │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Architecture  │ Client-Server (dockerd│ Daemonless (fork-exec model)  │
  │               │  daemon runs as root) │ No background process needed  │
  │ Root required │ Yes (dockerd is root) │ No — rootless by default!     │
  │ systemd       │ Not native           │ podman generate systemd → unit │
  │ Docker Compose│ docker compose up     │ podman-compose (or docker compose)│
  │ Image format  │ OCI (same)           │ OCI (same) — interoperable!   │
  │ CLI compat    │ docker run ...        │ alias docker=podman (99% same)│
  │ Kubernetes    │ Needs Docker Desktop  │ podman play kube (native YAML)│
  │ Best for      │ Dev machines, Mac/Win │ Production Linux servers       │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘
OpsDeploy Q42 of 200
In a Dockerfile for a web application, which instruction ensures the container does NOT run as root?
A) EXPOSE 8000 — non-root ports enforce non-root execution
B) WORKDIR /opt/anihpj — changing the working directory drops privileges
C) CMD ["gunicorn", "--user", "nobody", ...] — the application itself handles user switching
D) USER 1000 — switches to UID 1000 for all subsequent RUN, CMD, and ENTRYPOINT instructions; the container process runs as this user, not root
✅ Correct Answer: D

USER 1000 switches the container's runtime user to UID 1000. Without this, the container runs as root (UID 0) — a major security risk. If an attacker compromises the app, they have root inside the container (and potentially on the host if they escape).

💻 Dockerfile Example
$ cat /opt/anihpj/Dockerfile
FROM python:3.12-slim AS builder
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --user -r requirements.txt
FROM python:3.12-slim
COPY --from=builder /root/.local /home/anihpj/.local
COPY . /opt/anihpj/
RUN useradd -m -u 1000 anihpj
USER 1000
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"]
📝 Dockerfile Best Practices
Essential Dockerfile Instructions
  INSTRUCTION     PURPOSE                    EXAMPLE
  ──────────────  ─────────────────────────  ────────────────────────────────
  FROM            Base image                 FROM python:3.12-slim
  WORKDIR         Set working directory       WORKDIR /opt/app
  COPY            Copy files into image       COPY requirements.txt .
  RUN             Execute build commands      RUN pip install -r req.txt
  USER            Switch to non-root user     USER 1000
  EXPOSE          Document port (informational) EXPOSE 8000
  ENV             Set environment variable    ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
  CMD             Default runtime command     CMD ["gunicorn", "app:app"]
  ENTRYPOINT      Fixed entrypoint (CMD=args)  ENTRYPOINT ["/entrypoint.sh"]

  MULTI-STAGE BUILDS (smaller final image):
  FROM base AS builder → build deps → FROM base → COPY --from=builder
  Build tools (gcc, headers) stay in builder stage — final image is lean.

  ⚠️  RUN useradd BEFORE USER — create the user, then switch to it.
      NEVER run containers as root in production.
OpsDeploy Q43 of 200
Two containers need to communicate: anihpj-api needs to reach anihpj-db (PostgreSQL). How does Docker DNS-based service discovery work on a custom network?
