/etc/skel is a simple but powerful customization tool. Add useful defaults once, and every future user benefits. This is standard Linux administration practice for both servers and workstations.

UsersGroups Q189 of 200
How do you set persistent environment variables system-wide and per-user?
A) Use export VAR=value in the terminal — it persists across logins
B) Edit /etc/profile directly — it's the only system-wide configuration file
C) System-wide: /etc/environment (all users, not a script) or /etc/profile.d/myapp.sh (scripted). Per-user: ~/.bashrc (non-login) or ~/.profile (login). Use env or printenv to list current variables
D) Environment variables can only be set in systemd unit files — shell configs are ignored
✅ Correct Answer: C

Environment variables control PATH, language settings, application config, and more. /etc/profile.d/ is the clean way to add system-wide variables — drop a .sh file and it auto-loads. /etc/environment is simpler but limited (no scripting, just KEY=value).

� Detailed Explanation — Where to Set Environment Variables
Environment Variable Scopes — System-Wide to Per-User
  SCOPE              FILE                     TYPE        WHEN APPLIED
  ─────────────────  ───────────────────────  ──────────  ────────────────
  All users, all     /etc/environment         KEY=value   At login (PAM)
  shells (static)
  All users, bash    /etc/profile             Script      Login shells
  All users, modular /etc/profile.d/*.sh      Script      Login shells (auto)
  Per-user, login    ~/.bash_profile          Script      User's login shell
  Per-user, non-login ~/.bashrc               Script      New terminals, su
  Per-service        EnvironmentFile= in unit systemd     Service start
  LIST CURRENT: env | sort   or   printenv
�💻 Live Shell
$ cat /etc/profile.d/anihpj.sh
export APP_ENV=production
export DATABASE_URL=postgresql://localhost/anihpj

$ source /etc/profile.d/anihpj.sh
$ echo $APP_ENV
production
# All users get APP_ENV on login ✅

Environment variables are the standard way to configure applications on Linux. The LFCS exam tests your understanding of where to set them for different scopes (system-wide vs per-user, login vs non-login).

UsersGroups Q190 of 200
How do you securely configure sudo with visudo and restrict commands?
A) Edit /etc/sudoers directly with vim — it's the fastest method
B) sudo is all-or-nothing — you can't restrict specific commands
C) Add users to the sudo group — that's the only configuration needed
D) ALWAYS use visudo — it validates syntax before saving (a typo can lock you out!). %developers ALL=(ALL) /opt/anihpj/deploy.sh lets the developers group run ONLY that script as root. NOPASSWD: skips password prompt for automated scripts
✅ Correct Answer: D

visudo is MANDATORY for editing sudoers — it checks syntax and prevents lockouts. The principle of least privilege applies to sudo: grant access to specific commands, not blanket root access. NOPASSWD is useful for deployment scripts but dangerous for general use.

� Detailed Explanation — sudoers Syntax Reference
sudoers — Grant Specific Privileges
  FORMAT:  WHO    WHERE=(AS_WHO)  TAGS: COMMAND
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ %deploy  ALL=(ALL)  /opt/anihpj/deploy.sh                      │
  │ → Group deploy can run deploy.sh as any user on any host       │
  │                                                                 │
  │ alice    ALL=(ALL)  NOPASSWD: /bin/systemctl restart myapp     │
  │ → Alice can restart myapp without password                     │
  │                                                                 │
  │ bob      ALL=(root) /usr/bin/apt update, /usr/bin/apt install  │
  │ → Bob can only run apt update and apt install as root          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  RULES: ① ALWAYS use visudo (never edit /etc/sudoers directly)
         ② Put custom rules in /etc/sudoers.d/ (not the main file)
         ③ Test with: sudo -l (list allowed commands)
�💻 Live Shell
$ sudo visudo -f /etc/sudoers.d/anihpj
# Add:
%developers ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /opt/anihpj/deploy.sh
anihpj ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /bin/systemctl restart anihpj-api

$ sudo -l
User alice may run the following commands:
(ALL) NOPASSWD: /opt/anihpj/deploy.sh
# alice can deploy without a password ✅

sudo configuration is critical security infrastructure. Always use visudo, follow least privilege, and put custom rules in /etc/sudoers.d/ (not the main file). This is fundamental Linux security practice.

5.3 User Resource LimitsQ191-Q194

UsersGroups Q191 of 200
How do soft and hard limits work in /etc/security/limits.conf?
A) Soft and hard limits are the same — they're just two names for one value
B) Soft limit: current enforced limit. Hard limit: maximum the user can raise the soft limit to. Users can increase their own soft limit up to the hard limit without root. Root sets both. Configured per-user or per-group (@groupname)
C) Hard limits only apply to the root user — regular users only have soft limits
D) limits.conf is only for process limits — file limits are set elsewhere
✅ Correct Answer: B

The soft/hard distinction gives users flexibility without compromising security. Set the soft limit low for safety, hard limit higher for burst capacity. Users can temporarily raise their soft limit with ulimit -n 8192 when they need more, up to the hard ceiling.

� Detailed Explanation — Soft vs Hard Limits
limits.conf — Soft vs Hard Limit Behavior
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj   soft   nofile   4096    ← Current enforced limit       │
  │ anihpj   hard   nofile   16384   ← Ceiling (user can raise to)  │
  │                                                                 │
  │ USER CAN:  ulimit -n 8192      ✅  (below hard, above soft)     │
  │ USER CAN:  ulimit -n 16384     ✅  (equal to hard limit)        │
  │ USER CAN'T: ulimit -n 20000    ❌  (exceeds hard limit!)        │
  │ USER CAN'T: ulimit -n 2048     ✅  (decrease is always allowed) │
  │                                                                 │
  │ ROOT CAN:  Set any value, override all limits                   │
  │ SYSTEMD:   Use MemoryMax=, CPUQuota= for per-service limits     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
�💻 Live Shell
$ cat /etc/security/limits.conf | grep anihpj
anihpj soft nofile 4096
anihpj hard nofile 16384

$ ulimit -n 8192 # Raise to 8192 (below hard limit of 16384)
$ ulimit -n 20000 # FAILS — exceeds hard limit!
bash: ulimit: open files: cannot modify limit: Operation not permitted

Resource limits prevent runaway processes from exhausting system resources. Database servers especially need high nofile limits. This is standard Linux administration for any production server.

UsersGroups Q192 of 200
What does ulimit show and how is it different from limits.conf?
A) ulimit -a shows ALL limits for the current shell session. ulimit -n 4096 sets temporarily. Key difference: ulimit changes are SESSION-SCOPED (lost on logout), limits.conf is PERMANENT (applied at login via PAM). Non-root users can only decrease soft limits, never increase beyond hard
B) ulimit and limits.conf are identical — ulimit is just the CLI version of the same config
C) ulimit only shows memory limits — use sysctl for file and process limits
D) ulimit is deprecated — use prlimit on modern Linux distributions
✅ Correct Answer: A

ulimit is the quick check tool — use it to verify that limits.conf changes took effect after login. If ulimit shows different values than limits.conf, check that PAM is enabled (session required pam_limits.so in /etc/pam.d/).

💻 Live Shell
$ ulimit -a
core file size (blocks) 0
data seg size (kbytes) unlimited
open files (-n) 4096
max user processes (-u) 4096
locked memory (-l) 65536
# Current shell limits — check against limits.conf ✅

The ulimit → limits.conf workflow: set values in limits.conf, restart session, verify with ulimit. This is the standard way to configure resource limits on every Linux distribution.

📝 Resource Limits — Complete Setup
ulimit / limits.conf / systemd Limit*= — Three Ways
  METHOD 1: /etc/security/limits.conf (PAM login sessions)
  anihpj    soft    nofile    65536
  anihpj    hard    nofile    65536

  METHOD 2: systemd unit file (PREFERRED for services)
  [Service]
  LimitNOFILE=65536
  LimitNPROC=4096
  LimitMEMLOCK=infinity

  METHOD 3: sysctl (kernel-wide)
  fs.file-max = 2097152
  fs.nr_open = 1048576

  VERIFY: ulimit -n (current shell), cat /proc/PID/limits (running process)
UsersGroups Q193 of 200
How do you verify that PAM limits are enabled and actually being applied?
A) PAM limits are always enabled — no verification is needed
B) Check /etc/pam.d/limits.conf exists — that's all that's required
C) Verify session required pam_limits.so is present in /etc/pam.d/common-session (Debian/Ubuntu) or /etc/pam.d/system-auth (RHEL). Without this line, limits.conf is completely IGNORED. Test by setting a limit, logging out/in, and checking with ulimit -a
D) PAM is only for authentication — it doesn't affect resource limits
✅ Correct Answer: C

Without the pam_limits.so line, limits.conf is dead configuration. This is the #1 reason "I set limits.conf but nothing changed!" — the PAM module isn't loaded. SSH, login, and su all use PAM, so limits apply to all login methods.

💻 Live Shell
$ grep pam_limits /etc/pam.d/common-session
session required pam_limits.so
# PAM limits module is active ✅

$ ssh localhost 'ulimit -n'
4096
# Limit applied via SSH (uses PAM) ✅

PAM is the gatekeeper for all login-based access control. Understanding PAM configuration is essential for troubleshooting "my limits aren't working" issues on any Linux system.

📝 PAM — Pluggable Authentication Modules
PAM — How Limits, Passwords, and Access Are Enforced
  /etc/pam.d/ STRUCTURE:
  TYPE    CONTROL    MODULE          ARGUMENTS
  ──────  ─────────  ──────────────  ────────────
  auth    required   pam_unix.so     (password check)
  session required   pam_limits.so   (apply limits.conf!)
  account required   pam_access.so   (access.conf rules)

  CRITICAL: limits.conf only works if pam_limits.so is in the PAM stack!
  Check: grep pam_limits /etc/pam.d/common-session

  TROUBLESHOOT: "My limits aren't working"
  1. Check limits.conf syntax
  2. Verify pam_limits.so is in /etc/pam.d/common-session
  3. Log out and back in (limits are applied at login)
  4. For systemd services: use LimitNOFILE= in unit file instead
UsersGroups Q194 of 200
What resource limits should a production web application user typically have?
A) Unlimited everything — let the kernel manage resources
B) Only nofile needs tuning — everything else uses safe defaults
C) Set everything to 65536 — that's the standard production value
D) App user: nofile=8192 (database connections + sockets), nproc=4096 (workers + threads). Database user: nofile=65536 (many client connections), memlock=unlimited (shared_buffers). Always measure first, then set limits ~2× observed peak usage
✅ Correct Answer: D

Resource limits should match the workload. Database servers need very high nofile (each connection is a file descriptor). App servers need moderate nofile. Always measure peak usage with lsof | wc -l before setting limits — don't guess.

📝 Production Limit Recommendations
Recommended Resource Limits by Role
  ROLE           nofile    nproc     memlock      NOTES
  ────────────  ────────  ────────  ───────────  ──────────────────
  App server     8192      4096      default      ~100 conns + overhead
  Database      65536      16384     unlimited    Each connection = 1 FD
  Cache (Redis) 65536      4096      unlimited    Many client connections
  Proxy (Nginx) 65536      8192      default      Worker × connections
  Admin users    4096      4096      default      Interactive use only

Data-driven limits prevent production outages. The #1 cause of "too many open files" errors is insufficient nofile limits on database or proxy servers. This is infrastructure that applies to every application stack.

5.4 Access Control Lists (ACLs)Q195-Q198

UsersGroups Q195 of 200
What are ACLs (Access Control Lists) and what problem do they solve?
A) ACLs replace Unix permissions entirely — they're a completely different system
B) ACLs extend the traditional owner-group-others model by allowing per-user and per-group permissions beyond just the owning user/group. Example: give bob read access while the owning group is developers. getfacl reads, setfacl writes. Requires filesystem mounted with acl option
C) ACLs only work on NFS filesystems — local filesystems use standard permissions
D) ACLs are a Windows feature — they don't exist on Linux
✅ Correct Answer: B

ACLs solve the "I need to give one specific user access but they're not in the group" problem. Without ACLs, you'd need to create a new group, add both users, and chgrp the file. ACLs do it in one setfacl command.

� Detailed Explanation — ACL vs Standard Permissions
ACLs — Beyond Owner-Group-Others
  STANDARD PERMISSIONS (limited)          ACLS (flexible)
  ┌──────────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ owner: alice    rw-      │           │ user::rw-     (owner)        │
  │ group: devs     r--      │           │ user:bob:rw-  (extra user)   │
  │ other:          ---      │           │ group::r--    (owning group) │
  │                          │           │ group:audit:r (extra group)  │
  │ bob (not in devs) = ❌   │           │ mask::rw-     (max allowed)  │
  └──────────────────────────┘           │ other::---                   │
                                          └──────────────────────────────┘
  SET:  setfacl -m u:bob:rw file          CHECK: getfacl file
        setfacl -m g:auditors:r file             ls -l (shows + if ACLs)
�💻 Live Shell
$ setfacl -m u:bob:rw config.yaml
$ getfacl config.yaml
# file: config.yaml
# owner: alice
# group: developers
user::rw-
user:bob:rw-
group::r--
mask::rw-
other::---
# bob has rw- even though he's not in the developers group ✅

ACLs are essential for complex permission scenarios. The LFCS exam expects you to know how to read, set, and troubleshoot ACLs. Most modern filesystems (ext4, XFS) have ACLs enabled by default.

UsersGroups Q196 of 200
How do you add and remove ACL entries with setfacl?
A) setfacl -m u:alice:rw file (modify/add user). setfacl -m g:devs:rx dir (group). setfacl -x u:alice file (remove user entry). setfacl -b file (remove ALL ACLs). setfacl -R for recursive application
B) Edit /etc/acl.conf — ACLs are configured in a central file
C) ACLs are set via chmod +acl — it's an extension of the chmod command
D) ACLs can only be set during file creation — they can't be modified later
✅ Correct Answer: A

setfacl syntax: -m (modify), -x (remove specific), -b (remove all). The u: prefix is for users, g: for groups. -R applies recursively — essential for directory trees. ls -l shows a + sign when ACLs are present.

💻 Live Shell
$ ls -l config.yaml
-rw-r-----+ 1 alice developers 1024 Jun 14 config.yaml
# The + at the end means ACLs are present!

$ setfacl -m g:auditors:r config.yaml
$ setfacl -x u:bob config.yaml
# ACLs updated — auditors added, bob removed ✅

ACL management is a core sysadmin skill. The + in ls output is the quick way to spot ACLs. Always use getfacl (not ls) to see the complete permission picture.

📝 ACLs — Beyond Traditional Permissions
ACL Commands — getfacl & setfacl
  SPOT ACLs: ls -l shows "+" at end of permissions
  -rw-rwx---+ 1 anihpj developers 4096 Jun 13 file.txt

  VIEW: getfacl file.txt
  # file: file.txt
  # owner: anihpj
  # group: developers
  user::rw-
  user:bob:rwx       ← ACL: bob has full access
  group::r--
  mask::rwx
  other::---

  SET:
  setfacl -m u:bob:rwx file.txt     Grant bob rwx
  setfacl -m g:dba:r file.txt       Grant dba group read
  setfacl -x u:bob file.txt         Remove bob's ACL
  setfacl -b file.txt               Remove ALL ACLs
UsersGroups Q197 of 200
What are default ACLs and how do they enable automatic permission inheritance?
A) Default ACLs are applied system-wide — they affect all files on the server
B) Default ACLs are the same as regular ACLs — there's no inheritance concept
C) Default ACLs only work on the root filesystem — they don't apply to data volumes
D) setfacl -m d:g:devs:rwx /opt/anihpj/uploads/ sets a default ACL. Every NEW file and subdirectory created inside inherits these permissions automatically. Existing files are NOT affected — use setfacl -R to apply retroactively
✅ Correct Answer: D

Default ACLs solve the "new files don't get the right permissions" problem. Without them, every new file needs manual ACL setting. With default ACLs, the directory enforces permissions automatically on all future content.

💻 Live Shell
$ setfacl -m d:g:developers:rwx /opt/anihpj/uploads/
$ getfacl /opt/anihpj/uploads/
# file: /opt/anihpj/uploads/
user::rwx
group::r-x
other::r-x
default:user::rwx
default:group::r-x
default:group:developers:rwx
# New files will inherit developers:rwx ✅

Default ACLs are essential for shared directories. Set them once on the top-level directory, and every new upload, log, or config file gets the correct permissions automatically. This is production-grade file permission management.

📝 Default ACLs — Inherited Permissions
Default ACLs — Set Once, Apply to All New Files
  SET DEFAULT ACL:
  setfacl -dm u:bob:rwx,g:dba:r /opt/anihpj/uploads

  NOW: Every NEW file/dir in uploads/ automatically gets:
  • bob has rwx
  • dba group has r
  • Owner still has their normal permissions

  VERIFY:
  getfacl /opt/anihpj/uploads
  # default:user:bob:rwx
  # default:group:dba:r--

  USE CASE: Shared upload directory — web server user + developer both
  need access to new files without manually chmod-ing every upload.

  REMOVE: setfacl -k /opt/anihpj/uploads  (remove default ACL)
UsersGroups Q198 of 200
How would you use ACLs for a multi-admin web application with granular access control?
A) Use Unix groups only — ACLs are unnecessary complexity for web apps
B) ① App directory: owner=anihpj, group=developers (rwx). ② Config files: setfacl to give read-only to auditor user. ③ Uploads directory: default ACL for developers group (rwx inheritance). ④ Deploy script: ACL granting execute to deployer user specifically. Each role gets exactly what it needs — no more
C) Give everyone full access — trust your team
D) ACLs should only be used for system directories — application directories use standard permissions
✅ Correct Answer: B

ACLs enable true least-privilege access control. Auditors get read-only. Developers get write access to uploads. Deployers get execute on scripts. No one gets more than they need. This is the professional approach to multi-admin file permissions.

📝 ACL Strategy for Web Applications
Granular Permissions with ACLs
  DIRECTORY/FILE        STANDARD PERMS    ACL ADDITIONS           WHO BENEFITS
  ────────────────────  ────────────────  ──────────────────────  ────────────
  /opt/anihpj/          anihpj:devs:r-x  g:auditors:r-x          Auditors
  /opt/anihpj/config/   anihpj:devs:r-x  u:deployer:r-x          CI/CD system
  /opt/anihpj/uploads/  anihpj:devs:rwx  d:g:devs:rwx (inherit)  All developers
  deploy.sh             anihpj:devs:r-x  u:deployer:--x          Deployer only

ACLs complete the Linux permissions toolkit. Combined with standard Unix permissions, groups, and sudo, they enable precise, auditable access control for any application stack.

5.5 LDAP User & Group AccountsQ199-Q200

UsersGroups Q199 of 200
What is LDAP and why is it used for centralized authentication?
A) LDAP is a database for storing application data — it replaces PostgreSQL
B) LDAP is only for email servers — general Linux systems use local accounts
C) LDAP (Lightweight Directory Access Protocol) provides a centralized directory for users, groups, and authentication. One LDAP server can authenticate users across hundreds of Linux servers. Implementations: OpenLDAP, FreeIPA, Microsoft Active Directory. Linux connects via SSSD or nslcd
D) LDAP stores passwords in plaintext for fast authentication
✅ Correct Answer: C

LDAP eliminates the nightmare of managing local accounts on hundreds of servers. Create a user once in LDAP, and they can log into every server. Change a password once, and it updates everywhere. This is enterprise identity management.

📝 Centralized Auth Architecture
LDAP — One Identity, Many Servers
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    LDAP SERVER                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Users: alice, bob, charlie                           │   │
  │  │ Groups: developers, admins, auditors                 │   │
  │  │ Passwords: stored as hashes (SSHA, etc.)             │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┘
                         │
         ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐
         ▼               ▼               ▼
  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐
  │ Server 1 │   │ Server 2 │   │ Server N │
  │ (SSSD)   │   │ (SSSD)   │   │ (SSSD)   │
  │ alice can│   │ alice can│   │ alice can│
  │ log in!  │   │ log in!  │   │ log in!  │
  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘
  /etc/nsswitch.conf: passwd: files sss → check local THEN LDAP

LDAP is enterprise-standard identity management. The LFCS exam tests basic LDAP integration concepts. For production deployments, FreeIPA provides a complete solution with LDAP + Kerberos + certificate management.

UsersGroups Q200 of 200
How does SSSD connect Linux to LDAP for transparent authentication?
A) SSSD (System Security Services Daemon) connects to LDAP and caches credentials. Configure /etc/sssd/sssd.conf with LDAP server URI and search base. Set /etc/nsswitch.conf to passwd: files sss. Then getent passwd alice returns both local AND LDAP users transparently. id alice shows resolved groups
B) SSSD replaces /etc/passwd entirely — local accounts are migrated to LDAP
C) SSSD only works with Microsoft Active Directory — not with OpenLDAP
D) SSSD requires a GUI configuration tool — it can't be configured from the command line
✅ Correct Answer: A

SSSD is the modern bridge between Linux and enterprise identity providers. It handles authentication, caches credentials (so users can log in even if the LDAP server is temporarily down), and integrates with nsswitch for transparent user resolution.

� Detailed Explanation — SSSD Architecture
SSSD — Connecting Linux to LDAP/AD
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                      SSSD ARCHITECTURE                          │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ getent   │───▶│ nsswitch │───▶│ SSSD (cache + forward)   │  │
  │  │ passwd   │    │ passwd:  │    │                          │  │
  │  │ alice    │    │ files sss│    │ ┌────────────────────┐   │  │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    │ │ Local cache (fast) │   │  │
  │                                  │ └────────┬───────────┘   │  │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    │          │ (cache miss)  │  │
  │  │ ssh      │───▶│ PAM      │───▶│          ▼               │  │
  │  │ alice@.. │    │ auth: sss│    │ ┌────────────────────┐   │  │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    │ │ LDAP/AD server     │   │  │
  │                                  │ │ (authoritative)    │   │  │
  │                                  │ └────────────────────┘   │  │
  │                                  └──────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
�💻 Live Shell
$ cat /etc/sssd/sssd.conf
[sssd]
domains = example.com
[domain/example.com]
id_provider = ldap
auth_provider = ldap
ldap_uri = ldap://192.168.1.50
ldap_search_base = dc=example,dc=com

$ getent passwd alice
alice:*:2001:2001:Alice:/home/alice:/bin/bash
# alice resolved via LDAP through SSSD — not in /etc/passwd! ✅

SSSD is the standard way to connect Linux to enterprise identity providers. It provides offline authentication (credential caching), integrates with nsswitch, and supports multiple identity sources simultaneously. This is enterprise Linux administration.
